- **#767 — the two fall-backs keep the last good answer for ten minutes instead of making the heavy read: matter 4 of the Postgres war (20 Nov; handbook Part 123; `api/_lib/supabase.js`, `api/_lib/call-core.js`, `api/_lib/portal-core.js`, server only, `BUILD` and `CALL_BUILD` unchanged).** "postgress war please; and that shouldnt distract efficiency of system operations" — the owner, 20 Nov (section 2); CLAUDE.md section 1. **From the second `pg_stat_statements` paste (#766):** the OLD reads that RUN-ME-077 and RUN-ME-078 replaced still ran as often as the answers (917 s, 47% of the listed database time in 3 h 43 min), because `calledTodaySet` and `callBoardSource_` treated EVERY failure of the small answer alike: one failed call (a statement cancelled at the role's limit, a full connection pool, a network error) meant five minutes of the heavy read for that server instance, made when the database was already busy. **What was built:** one rule in two places, on the definition the reader already retries on (`isTransient`) [CHANGED in #769: a stricter test of its own, `isBusyFailure`] and one constant (`STALE_OK_MS`, ten minutes, `supabase.js`): **when the small answer fails and the failure is transient, the last good answer stands in for up to ten minutes (counted from when it was built, never renewed by standing in) and the heavy read is not made.** Every other case is answered by the old read exactly as before: a function not installed, refused, answering a shape it does not make, a scope or instance with no good answer, a failure that has lasted ten minutes. The called-today set is the instance's own entry for today (its own syncs go on adding to it; the threshold is NOT compared, because it is read from the same struggling database and a failed read answers the default); the board keeps one answer per viewer's scope (week, today, teams) and the answer of `officerBoards` carries `callsStaleS` (seconds) only when the boards are an older answer. **Cost (CLAUDE.md section 1):** `/api/call`: no read added, reads fewer (twelve minutes of a failing database cost ONE read of the day where each refresh cost one) [CORRECTED, #769 and Part 126: not true of the called-today path while a set stood in: each list request still made one `settings` read (the threshold); fixed: nothing is read while a held set stands in]; `/api/upload`: nothing; `test/speed.test.mjs` unchanged. **Checked:** 17 new tests; the new behaviour tests fail on the old source (6 of 16 called-today, 4 of 6 board); **31 deliberate breaks each turn a test red** (30 by behaviour tests alone; the board's memory pruning only by its source pin, because the age check makes it invisible from outside); `npm test` 1,954 pass. **Mistakes of mine (Part 123):** my first form compared the set's threshold and would have sent the heavy read exactly when the settings read had failed in the same stall (found reading my own diff, taken out); three breaks were caught only by the pins at first (a thrown fault, the day in the scope key masked by the age, the pruning) and the tests were strengthened; two of my own test expectations were wrong at first (the one-minute answer cache; the pause belongs to the whole process). **Not done, said plainly:** the failures themselves are not cured (the Logs Explorer answers name the statements); a request that finds the set expired still waits for the failing function (up to about 8 s) before the stand-in is served; a cold instance with no set still reads the day once; the screen does not draw the age of a board; nothing live seen; not read by a second reader. **To see the effect:** the verification query again in a few hours (section 8). **Also in #767: (a) a failed small answer now says why in the server's own log** (`errWords`; `[call] ...` and `[boards] ...`: one line for each failure, never on a good answer, nothing from the five minutes of pause; in Vercel, the project's Logs, search `did not answer`: it will tell a timeout from a full pool from the network; the suite stays quiet for the two tags in `test/fake-db.mjs`), 20 new tests, 39 deliberate breaks each caught. **(b) The Logs Explorer queries of #766 are DROPPED: the owner ran them in the SQL editor three times (`42P01 relation "edge_logs"/"postgres_logs" does not exist`), so two read-only SQL files that run where they work replace them (handbook Part 124):** `db/RUN-ME-079-database-health-read-only.sql` (how the database is doing) and `db/RUN-ME-080-statements-that-spill-read-only.sql` (which statements write to temp files and read the disk most), with `test/database-health.test.mjs` and the kept check `tools/db-checks/database-health.mjs` (18 + 5 checks on a PostgreSQL 16 scratch server, 6 breaks each caught). **The owner ran RUN-ME-079 at 18:23 East Africa time:** a 2 GB machine (`shared_buffers` 512 MB), a 2.86 GB database whose defaulter decks table alone is 1.98 GB (92.5% of its table pages from memory; the rest of the book 97.8 to 100%), cache hit 99.76% overall, 39,827 of 27.1 million transactions rolled back (0.147%, since the counters began: no rate yet), **143,120 temp files and 1,640 GB written to disk (about 570 times the size of the database: statements spilling; the old paged reads are the likely cause, not known)**, deadlocks 0, the app's role under an 8-second statement and lock timeout (the authenticator's), and two repair logs of 46 and 20 MB the app never reads. **Not concluded:** whether the compute size is the limit; which statements spill (RUN-ME-080 is next).
- **#766 — the owner's second `pg_stat_statements` paste (20 Nov, 17:42 East Africa time) read and recorded, and the Logs Explorer queries handed over (handbook Part 120, addendum; docs only).** "postgress war please; and that shouldnt distract efficiency of system operations" — the owner, 20 Nov (section 2). They pasted two results with no words, 223 minutes after the first paste of the kind (13:58 East Africa time), and with them the error of the third query (run in the SQL editor, where `postgres_logs` does not exist: nothing ran; the second time). **What the two pastes say** (statements that error, a timeout among them, are NOT counted by `pg_stat_statements`: every figure is for statements that finished): the new called-today answer takes 31 ms (386 runs, 142 ms at worst) where the old read took 320 ms and 8 s at worst; the ghost lookup takes 86 ms a run where it took 240 (709 runs) and never passes its own 800 ms; the phones' inserts are unchanged; the statements in the table add up to about 1,945 s of database time in 13,405 s of clock (a lower bound, not a load figure). **The finding: the old reads are still running about as often as the new answers.** The only readers of those two statement shapes are the fall-backs (`calledTodaySet`, `callBoardSource_`): the old called-today read ran 389 times beside 386 runs of the new answer, the old week read 810 times (about 58 boards) beside 31 runs of the rollup; together 917 s, 47% of the listed time in the window. When the small answer fails once, the app makes the heavy read for five minutes, exactly when the database is struggling. **Why it fails is not known** (a failed statement is not in the table: a timeout, a wait for a connection when the PostgREST pool is full of slow reads, or a network error would each fit); the rollup itself takes 3.0 s on average and 5.5 s at worst against a limit of about 8 s. **Two corrections:** the 560-run statement `(p_dates, p_teams, p_lookback)` (2,659 ms on average, 7,996 ms at worst) did not run once in the window and is declared in no file of the repository (an older function): the first reading took it for the dashboard card's function, wrong; and the Find customer search (3.3 s on average when it runs) ran 5 times, probably slow on phone-number searches (no trigram index on `contact`: probable, not measured; an index costs the upload, so it is measured before it is offered). **Handed over in the chat:** two queries for Supabase, Logs, **Logs Explorer** (not the SQL editor; the address ending `/logs/explorer`; "Last 24 hours"): the API Gateway's answers of 500 and above by path and status, and the Postgres `statement timeout` rows grouped by statement text; and a screenshot of Reports, Database. **(Dropped the same evening, #767: the owner ran them in the SQL editor three times; replaced by two read-only SQL files that run there.)** **Cost:** none: words only, no `BUILD` bump. **Not done, said plainly:** no code changed here; the statements behind the 7 to 8 timeouts a minute are not named; the cause of the failures that trigger the fall-backs is not known; the timeouts per minute since RUN-ME-075 to 078 are not known. **Next, from this reading:** matter 4, the two fall-backs keep the last good answer for a few minutes instead of making the heavy read (Part 123).
- **#765 — the second reader of #764 read it: Undo now sticks, one rule for a cell that changes hands on both roads, names with an apostrophe or an accent are one person, failures and results are said, an empty-name code is no longer shown as a name (20 Nov; handbook Part 122; `api/_lib/leaders-sync.js`, `api/_lib/portal-core.js`, `api/_lib/holders.js`, `public/mod/admin.js`, `public/app.html`; `BUILD 2026-11-20-d`, `CALL_BUILD` unchanged).** "please never stop until whats asked is done." — the owner, 20 Nov (section 2); the rule of section 7 (merge first, then one reader, nothing owed). **The reader** (one, after #764 merged and was live on both sites; its own scripts: the real `portalApi` over the fake database, and the real page in a Chromium against the real `portalApi`) reported; **every finding was reproduced by hand on the merged code** before it was believed. **What was true:** (1) **Undo could not stick**: the screen's next look found the same difference and wrote it again (the real page's calls after the click: `leadersSyncUndo, leadersCheck, leadersSync`); Part 121 and #764's entry said "Undo is the way back": false as merged, and the stub of the first browser check could not show it. (2) Rule 5 (a new person does not inherit the last leader's number and ID) did not hold on the road people use most, the save of ONE access code. (3) A bare short form (JOHN) lent its number and ID to a different person (JOHN BETA). (4) The same person written with an apostrophe, an accent or in another alphabet counted as a new person and had the number and ID blanked. (5) Failures were silent (the screen swallowed a failed check or sync; the code form ignored what the sync did; a first sync run by a code save never reached the card). (6) `leadersCheck` was missing from `READ_FNS`. (7) Smaller: Undo put back a whole cell when only the name still matched (a number typed since was lost); twin team rows (`KONGOWE`, `KONGOWE `) restored one; **an access code with no name was shown as the Collection or Legal officer's NAME on every team it holds, to every viewer of the Teams tab: that name is a password**; one person with two codes was listed as "off their code"; a sync cut short left a log that the next one overwrote; equal-after-capitals codes wrote a spelling that depended on order. **What was built:** **Undo holds** (plan rule 8): it leaves a hold (team, role, the person the code named) on each cell it puts back, written BEFORE the first cell, so the next look and a plain save of the same code do not write it back; a hold ends when no code names that person there any more or on **Sync now** (which says first that it will write what was undone, and sends `{releaseHolds:true}`); one rule for a cell that changes hands (`handsOverPatch`: the same words only, a value every such cell agrees on, else nothing), used by the plan and by `syncStaffFromCode`, which now also sends rows with different keys in different statements; `wordsOf` drops accents, drops an apostrophe instead of splitting on it, reads any alphabet, and a name with no words is nobody's "same person"; the same words written another way make the sheet follow the code (`respelt`), a SHORTER spelling is still kept; Undo puts a cell back only if everything the sync wrote there is still there (the log now keeps `wrote`; an old log is undone by the name cell), by the exact team spelling; a cut-short sync (`done:false`) is taken up by the next one and a look with nothing to do closes it; the screen says a failure (a card with the server's words and Try again; the list still opens), lists what is held and the cells of the last sync, and the save and the delete of a code say what the sheet did; **`deleteAccessCode` now brings the sheet into line right away** (that is how a stale code is put right); a code with no name names nobody. **Cost (CLAUDE.md section 1):** nothing on `/api/upload` or `/api/call` (the reader traced the imports); opening Teams & Staff reads the teams, the codes and ONE settings query (log and holds together: one wave less than before); a sync that changed something writes the log, the cells and the log again (closed); saving a code reads the holds once more; `test/speed.test.mjs` unchanged. **Checked:** `test/leaders-sync.test.mjs` 57 tests (21 of the 23 new ones fail on the merged code), `tools/browser-checks/portal-leaders-real.mjs` (new, the real page against the real `portalApi`, 41 points, **19 fail on the merged code**) beside the stub check (31 points); **51 of 51 deliberate breaks** each turn a test or the browser check red (three were missed at first and the tests were strengthened); `npm test` green. **Mistakes of mine (Part 122):** "Undo is the way back" was written and tested with a stub that could not show it was not; rule 5 on one road; `looseSame`, built for a history, taken as strict enough to hand a number over on; two of the new tests' own expectations were wrong at first. **Recorded, not changed:** a STALE code (a person who left, code never deleted) is written onto its teams at every open and `access_codes` has no inactive column: delete the code or take the teams off it (Undo now holds, so the cells can be put back first); role words such as "BIKE RECOVERY" name the Recovery column (the words the boards' overlay always read); the same `name || code` fallback exists at other sites (blank names only by SQL), not touched. **Not done, said plainly:** nothing live seen; the staff registry is still not consulted (an Inactive person is still written if their code names a team); not read by a second reader.
- **#764 — Teams & Staff leaders follow the access codes, always, for all the current data right away: `leadersSync` (20 Nov; handbook Part 121; `api/_lib/leaders-sync.js`, `api/_lib/portal-core.js`, `api/_lib/audit.js`, `api/_lib/holders.js`, `api/me.js`, `public/mod/admin.js`, `public/app.html`; `BUILD 2026-11-20-c`, `CALL_BUILD` unchanged).** The owner's order, in their words, is the 20 Nov row of section 2 ("The access codes hold the latest staff / leader per team ... must always update the leaders as from settings ... and that should affect all the current teams and staff nav data right away"). **What was already there:** `saveAccessCode` carried the ONE code just saved across to the sheet (for roles that are a column: GMO, MANAGER, OPM, RECOVERY ...); the boards laid the codes over the sheet for three units, never written back; the list filled a blank collection/legal from the codes and a typed name won; the history of who held what is recorded around every save. **What was missing:** nothing carried ALL the codes, so the rotation, the phone and the SMS lists, which read the STORED sheet, and both Teams & Staff screens (the list of team leaders and the staff by person) never showed what the codes say; and two definitions of "which column does this role name" existed. **What was built:** one column rule (`leaderColumnOf_`); a pure plan (`planLeaderSync`: a cell no code names keeps what Teams & Staff says; an all-teams code or a role that is no column names nothing; **two codes naming different people on one team are reported, never decided**; the same person is never re-spelt shorter; a new leader does not inherit the last one's number or ID; only columns the book has; order-independent); `leadersSync` (write, preview option) and `leadersSyncUndo` (the log is written BEFORE the first cell; no access code in it; audited, refused to a view-only code, recorded in `team_holders`); **it runs as the Teams & Staff screen opens** (the screen asks a READ first, `leadersCheck`, never audited and open to a view-only admin, and asks the write `leadersSync` only when it found a difference and only for a code that may change things, BEFORE it reads the list; `/api/me` now says `readOnly`), after every `saveAccessCode`, and on Sync now; a card at the top says what changed, what was kept, what two codes dispute, who is on teams their own code does not hold, with Sync now and Undo; the list's collection/legal officer now comes from the code over a typed name. **What the owner will see:** the first time an admin opens Teams & Staff after the deploy (or saves any code), every leader cell a code names is brought into line and listed (team, role, was, now); **customers move between leaders' lists wherever a code names somebody other than the sheet did, in the rotation, reports, commission and the phone: that is the order, and the card and `team_holders` are the record; Undo is the way back (corrected by #765: as merged, Undo did NOT stick).** **Cost (CLAUDE.md section 1):** nothing on `/api/upload` or `/api/call`; Teams & Staff as an admin reads the teams and codes it read anyway plus one settings row and writes only differences. **Checked:** `test/leaders-sync.test.mjs` (34 tests), `tools/browser-checks/portal-leaders-sync.mjs` (a real Chromium on the real page: 31 points, among them that the screen WAITS for the sync, a hostile leader name is drawn as text, a view-only code is shown what differs and never asked to write), three existing tests the rule touched; **34 of 34 deliberate breaks each turn a test or the browser check red**; `npm test` green. **Mistakes of mine (Part 121):** two definitions of one rule nearly left side by side; an all-teams test that passed with its own break; a screen-order break that was no break. **Not done, said plainly:** nothing live seen (the first sync's list is the owner's to review); a code changed by upload or by hand reaches the sheet at the next open or save; - **#763 — the phone index's lists live thirty minutes once their upload has settled: matter 3c of the Postgres war (20 Nov; handbook Part 120; `api/_lib/call-core.js`, `api/_lib/portal-core.js`, `api/followup.js`, server only, `BUILD` and `CALL_BUILD` unchanged).** "please never stop until whats asked is done." and "postgress war please; and that shouldnt distract efficiency of system operations" — the owner, 20 Nov (section 2); CLAUDE.md section 1. **What it was:** after #761 the four big reads of the phone index (the register, both sheets, the two-week window `expected_phone_window`) were still kept five minutes by every warm instance whatever happened (a "backstop"), and nothing said how many live rebuilds were that. **Why five:** DATA_VERSION is stamped on an upload's LAST slice and the register is written just AFTER the stamp, so a rebuild at the stamp can hold an unfinished register. **What was built:** the stamp is read as a clock (the upload's own `Date.now()`): a lists build made at least TEN minutes after it (`PHONE_INDEX_SETTLE_MS`) is SETTLED and kept THIRTY minutes (`PHONE_INDEX_SETTLED_TTL_MS`); a build made sooner is unsettled and lives five, as before; a stamp that is not a millisecond clock never settles; the numbered comments stay five minutes and a recorded number still re-reads only them; a new stamp rebuilds at the next sync. **What it rests on, read not assumed:** every writer of what the lists hold moves a stamp (an upload on its last slice; the comment writers); **two did not and now do**: the admin button that restores the register from the deck (`rebuildFollowup`, now moves DATA_VERSION after it writes) and the old `/api/followup` endpoint (no caller; now moves NEW_NUMBER_VERSION); `test/phone-index.test.mjs` reads the sources and fails if that stops being true. **The price:** a change made BY HAND in the database that moves no stamp reaches a phone's index after thirty minutes, not five; after such a script, in a new tab: `update settings set value = (extract(epoch from now()) * 1000)::bigint::text where key = 'DATA_VERSION';` (tested on a scratch database). **Cost (CLAUDE.md section 1):** no read added; a quiet settled instance builds the lists twice an hour instead of twelve times, and three or four times in the forty minutes after an upload instead of eight; nothing on `/api/upload`; `test/speed.test.mjs` unchanged. **The live saving is NOT known.** **Checked:** eleven new tests in `test/phone-index.test.mjs` (27 in all; the settled life, the exact settle point, six stamps that never settle, the price pinned at 29 and 30 minutes, the settled index equal to the old code's on 30 random books, the writers) and one in `test/portal.test.mjs`; **15 of 15 deliberate breaks each turn a test red**; `npm test` green. **Mistakes of mine (Part 120):** a break that changed my own comment and was "missed"; a condition around the restore's stamp that could never be false, removed. **Not done, said plainly:** a cold instance and the first sync after every upload still pay the whole index (a shared copy through the database, or one long-lived process: architecture, the owner's); nothing live seen; not read by a second reader.
- **#762 — the second reader of #759 read it: one real flaw fixed (an officer whose typed name is a key every object has wrote onto `Object.prototype`), the function's shape check made strict, a false claim corrected, and `db/RUN-ME-078` recorded as RUN (20 Nov; handbook Part 119; `api/_lib/portal-core.js`, server only, `BUILD` and `CALL_BUILD` unchanged).** "please never stop until whats asked is done." and "postgress war please; and that shouldnt distract efficiency of system operations" — the owner, 20 Nov (section 2). **RUN-ME-078 landed:** the owner pasted `officers_this_week | officers_today` = `382 | 285` (20 Nov): the function is live on the book and the Dashboard and Presentation call boards now come from it (the old read stands behind it as the fall-back); not seen from here, and the combined `pg_stat_statements` query is still to be run again in a few hours. **The reader** (started after the #759 merge, section 7: merge first; its own scratch PostgreSQL, the repository and git untouched, no `npm test`): the blanks over all 1,114,110 code points (JavaScript `trim()` and the SQL `btrim`: the same 25), about 2,000 fuzz books by 6 parameter sets on two collations (0 differences), and the whole `officerBoards` screen in three modes, 220 books by 8 viewers: 1,664 boards, 0 differences; the fall-back pauses and never blanks. **Checked by hand before being called real (section 7):** (1) **the flaw is real**: `callBoard` keys the officers on a plain object through `bucket()` (`if (!map[key])`), and an officer's name is free text typed at registration; reproduced through the real screen: one logged call under the name `__proto__` leaves `({}).team` reading that officer's team for every object in the server process until it restarts, and the officer is on no board (the board before #759 threw for such a name; after it, silent). **Fixed:** `bucket()` looks among the map's OWN properties and makes the key with `defineProperty` (`__proto__` gets a property of its own); every other key is the same property in the same order, so the 30 other callers are unchanged. (2) **The shape check was loose** (`Number(null)` is 0): all eight figures must now BE numbers and the name text. (3) **A claim of mine was false**: "never heavier than before" (#759's code comment, `db/RUN-ME-078`'s header line, Part 116, the PR body): a function that TIMES OUT is waited for until the role's limit (about 8 s) before the old read, once for each server instance and then not for five minutes; the pause is for the whole process, not one scope. Corrected in the comment, the header (a comment line; the function is untouched) and Part 116; the code is not changed, because the old read is the heavier of the two. **Recorded, NOT changed:** an outcome spelt `mißed` (JavaScript makes MISSED, PostgreSQL's libc leaves the `ß`: unreachable, `call_logs_outcome_check` and the sync's whitelist refuse it); `callRollupRows` joins its group key with an unescaped control character, so two names carrying it can fall into one group in JavaScript (the SQL is the exact one; the function is shared by Ripoti, the Calls tab and the phone's summary, so it is not touched for a case that needs a control character typed into a name); the old paged read could count a row twice if the phones inserted between pages (inference); the answer scales with distinct officer names (about 300 live). **Cost (CLAUDE.md section 1):** nothing on `/api/upload` or `/api/call`; on the portal `bucket()` makes one own-property test and a `defineProperty` for a key it has not seen. **Checked:** two tests in `test/call-board-rollup.test.mjs`, both red on the code as merged and green on the fix; **7 of 7 deliberate breaks each turn a test red** (the last was missed at first: the strict figures had made the old no-name test pass for another reason, and the test was strengthened); `npm test` green. **Mistakes of mine (Part 119):** the false claim; the pollution was reachable from the day #759 merged and the reader found it, not I; the no-name test lost its force. **Not done, said plainly:** the two recorded differences above; the pause is per process; nothing live seen; whether the boards or the statements look different is the owner's to say.
- **#761 — the phone index is read in two parts and built once: a recorded number costs one small read instead of seven, and syncs in flight together share one set of reads (20 Nov; handbook Part 118; `api/_lib/call-core.js`, server only, `BUILD` and `CALL_BUILD` unchanged).** "postgress war please; and that shouldnt distract efficiency of system operations" — the owner, 20 Nov (section 2); and CLAUDE.md section 1 (the call app never slows down). **What it was:** `phoneIndex` (what `/api/call`'s sync asks to tell a customer's number from a guarantor's, and what the phone's search reads) was ONE thing: five reads, seven statements (the follow-up register; today's and tomorrow's sheet, a date and its rows each; the two-week window `expected_phone_window`, the third heaviest statement of the owner's `pg_stat_statements` at 149,469 runs and 50,631 s; the numbered comments), kept per server instance against `today + DATA_VERSION + NEW_NUMBER_VERSION` and for five minutes, and thrown away whole when any of those moved. **What was found:** the two stamps do not mean the same thing (an upload changes the register, the sheets and the window; a recorded "Ana namba nyingine" changes only the numbered comments), yet one recorded number made every warm instance read the whole book again; and an instance handed several syncs at once, with the cache empty, ran the same reads once for each. **What was built:** the LISTS (four big reads) kept against today and `DATA_VERSION`, the NUMBERED COMMENTS (one small read) against `NEW_NUMBER_VERSION`, each for the same five minutes; the index made again from the two by arithmetic when either moves (`phoneIndexLists`, `phoneNumberRows`, `phoneIndexBuild`); a read in flight is shared (`phonePart_`), a failed read is forgotten at once, and the failure of an older read never discards a newer one; both parts start together as the five reads did; `phoneIndex(db, nowMs, dataVersion, numberVersion)` is exported and takes the stamps apart. **Cost (CLAUDE.md section 1):** no read added anywhere; a cold instance reads what it always read (7); a recorded number is ONE read where there were seven, for every warm instance; syncs in flight together are one set of reads; a served index none; nothing on `/api/upload`; `test/speed.test.mjs` unchanged; memory: the raw lists are kept beside the index (about as much again as the index, ESTIMATED at ten to twenty MB, not measured). **The live saving is NOT known:** it depends on what makes the instances rebuild today (recorded numbers, the five minutes, cold starts), which the statistics cannot tell apart. **Checked:** `test/phone-index.test.mjs` (17 tests): the OLD compute kept word for word as a YARDSTICK and compared with the new index key by key and in insertion order over 120 random books, 30 books without the window function and 60 random sequences of 40 events (uploads, recorded numbers, the clock, midnight): 2,400 syncs; the reads counted (cold 7, served 0, recorded number 1, upload 6, five minutes 7, twelve syncs at once one build, a failed build told to every waiter and not kept, an older failure leaving a newer build alone, a backwards clock not trusted, the two parts started together); **20 of 20 deliberate breaks each turn a test red** (and the existing end-to-end test of the recorded number catches a sync that passes the stamps joined); `npm test`: 1,857 pass. **Mistakes of mine (Part 118):** three, all in the tests, found at their first run (a failure count that forgot the reader retries a timeout; a caller pin cut at the first closing bracket; a counter built after the cache was filled, and the cache is kept against the client). **Not done, said plainly:** (1) the five minutes are unchanged: a longer life for the lists once their upload has had time to finish is the next matter (it is a freshness decision: the stamp moves just BEFORE the register is written, so a rebuild must still follow it), not taken here; (2) a cold instance still pays the whole index: only a copy shared through the database, or one long-lived process, cuts that (architecture, the owner's); (3) the window and register statements cost what they cost; (4) nothing live seen; (5) not read by a second reader (the yardstick, the counts and the breaks stand in).

So the ghost lookup's timeouts are its own 800 ms limit, and a lookup that needs longer (a viewer who holds many teams, probably) is cancelled every time. **What was built (matter 1):** `db/RUN-ME-077` adds `called_phones_today(p_day, p_min)`, one jsonb value `{"phones": [...]}` of the day's distinct numbers whose call lasted longer than `p_min`; on `/api/call` it REPLACES the day's read (one round trip where there were three for a day of 20,000 calls); before the file is run, one cheap failed call every five minutes per server instance, answered from PostgREST's schema cache (should reach neither Postgres nor its log); every row in a random place 25,860 pages, 119 to 164 ms against 8,780 pages, 23 to 34 ms, 91 KB: **1.9 to 2.9 times fewer pages, 4 to 8 times less database time, 8.5 times fewer bytes; the live numbers are NOT known.** **Checked:** seven new tests in `test/call.test.mjs` (**11 of 11 deliberate breaks of the app code caught**); `tools/db-checks/called-phones.mjs` (the function's answer equals the mock the tests read and, through `pnorm`, the old read's set on 200 random books; the pages in two layouts; the file as a file, who may call it, the undo; **11 of 11 deliberate breaks of the file caught**); `test/called-phones.test.mjs` (8 pins; **54 of 54 breaks of the pins' targets caught**); `npm test`: 1,821 pass. **The index of RUN-ME-076:** the owner built it (valid, 126 MB: about twice the 55 MB expected, so the live table holds about twice the current rows assumed); a second "Success. No rows returned" in that paste could have been the undo, so one line was asked; **the owner's answer was two bare "Success. No rows returned", read as "the check found no row, then the create line was run and succeeded": probably in place again, NOT seen; the one-line check is asked again (one row, `valid = true`).** **Mistakes of mine (Part 115):** the 800 ms claim; the first check measured the old read without the `ORDER BY id` the app adds; two of eleven breaks were not caught at first (an equivalent mutant, and a revoke a scratch database without Supabase's default grants cannot show); "26% of the database's time" was 27% of the twelve heaviest statements put together. **Not done, said plainly:** RUN-ME-077 not run (the live saving is not known); the called set keeps its half-minute life (a longer one is a product choice: a colleague's call would tick later); matters 2 to 4 (the dashboard's week of call logs, the two window functions, the follow-up list) come one at a time, each merged and reported; the index's state, the per-minute count of timeouts, the region and "Max rows" (section 8).
- **#756 — the partial index that cuts the ghost lookup's pages (`db/RUN-ME-076`, offered, NOT run), the kept check and pins for it and for RUN-ME-075, and RUN-ME-075's header corrected (20 Nov; handbook Part 114, addendum; DB files and tests only, no `BUILD` change).** The owner's order is section 2 (20 Nov, "postgress war ..."). **Counting pages found that #754 did not do what its header said:** the cheaper function reads the SAME pages as the first text (16,032 and 16,027 for one team and 297,974 and 297,972 for twenty on a book of 848,000 deck rows with 110 days of history for each customer, every row in a random place), because both read every row of every departed customer to find its newest current row; it saves CPU only. **What cuts the pages is `db/RUN-ME-076`: one partial index on the current rows, `(team, ref, snapshot_date desc, created_at desc) where snapshot_type = 'current'`, which makes a customer's newest current row one index descent and one row.** The same call with the index: 16,027 to 2,681 buffers for one team and 297,972 to 46,712 for twenty (6 to 6.4 times fewer); on the kept check's 60-team book 28,000 to 39,000 down to about 2,850 for four teams (10 to 14 times; the book is random), the lookup 4.0 pages for a customer whether it stood on the decks 30 days or 110 (without the index 15 to 25 against 68 to 95); the first text hardly profits (23,000 to 34,000), so it is RUN-ME-075 WITH RUN-ME-076 that works. **What it costs the upload (written beside it, CLAUDE.md section 1):** each CURRENT row an upload inserts writes one more entry (it is partial; initial rows do not): a deck day of 20,000 rows, 10,000 current, took 595 ms without it and 714 ms with it (median of ten interleaved runs: +119 ms, about 12 microseconds a current row); six runs of the check on a smaller book gave +0% to +21%; the live table has more indexes, so the share is smaller; about 55 MB on the live table. Built CONCURRENTLY (no block on the upload), run alone, undone by one drop. **Offered with those numbers, NOT run: the owner decides.** **Checked:** `tools/db-checks/ghost-rows-cheaper.mjs` on a PostgreSQL made from the whole real schema, the first text installed beside the new function: the answers equal the first text and a model written again on 250 random books and 11 hand-made cases (the same customer in two teams, initial rows only, a newest row that is an initial one, a day uploaded twice, an exact tie, empty and unknown teams, known refs matched exactly, a team asked twice, and the two inputs the app never sends, a NULL known list and a NULL inside it, pinned to what they do); the pages on a random-layout book of 60 teams; the index file as a file (alone, CONCURRENTLY, harmless twice, valid, named by the planner with no Sort node, undone by its drop); the cost of the insert; **21 of 21 deliberate breaks of the two files each make a check fail**; `test/ghost-rows-cheaper.test.mjs` (11 pins, among them the hash of the function that RAN: the repository cannot say the owner ran something else; **29 of 29 breaks of the pins caught**). **Mistakes of mine, in Part 114's addendum:** #754's "halves the cost" came from milliseconds on a warm book; the first form of the check used eight teams and the planner read the whole table for the first text; a harness key bug made every answer empty for a while. **Not done:** RUN-ME-076 not run; nothing live seen; the walk over a team's refs (about three buffers for each distinct ref) is the next cost; the 1,000-row cap, the refresh rate and the region (section 8).
- **#755 — the ghost lookup keeps the last good list when the database refuses it or is late; RUN-ME-075 ran (20 Nov; handbook Part 114; server only, `BUILD` and `CALL_BUILD` unchanged).** "postgress war please; and that shouldnt distract efficiency of system operations" — the owner, 20 Nov (section 2). **The owner ran `db/RUN-ME-075` (#754) at about 12:50 East Africa time and pasted its last line, `defaulter_ghost_rows`: the whole text ran (the editor sends a script as one statement, so an error anywhere would have shown instead) and the cheaper body is live. Its effect on the timeouts is NOT known yet.** Two read-only measurements were pasted in the chat: the `statement timeout` rows per minute for the last hour, in the Logs Explorer (7 to 8 a minute before), and the heaviest statements from `pg_stat_statements`, in the SQL editor (normalised text, no customer data; tested as one statement on a scratch database that loads the extension). **What the app did when the database refused (read in `api/_lib/expdf.js`):** a read of the ghost list that failed (a statement timeout is the usual way) threw away what the last good read had found and answered nothing for five minutes, so every customer who had left the book vanished from the Exp.Def lists and the phone's list for five minutes at a time, for whoever that server instance and team set served: the thing the owner ordered against on 13, 15 and 16 Nov. A read that was only late at the end of its budget did the same. **Now** the failure, the pause after it and the late read all hand out the last good list, however old (never made up; `ghostRowsFor_` still takes out every ref today's own deck names); with no good read behind it (RUN-ME-055 not run, a server instance that has just started) the answer is nothing, as before; the five-minute pause is unchanged (it is what stops a function that times out from being run, and timing out, over and over). Also fixed: the cache entry is written BEFORE the read starts, so a read that fails the moment it is asked for is remembered (the old order overwrote that memory with a settled promise and an instance then asked for nothing until it was restarted; the real client cannot fail that way, so nothing live changes). **Cost:** none: no read added, no round trip changed (`test/speed.test.mjs` unchanged), nothing on `/api/upload`; on `/api/call` arithmetic on a list the instance already holds. **Checked:** `test/ghost-lookup.test.mjs` (7 tests on a fake clock; 5 of the 7 fail on the code as it was, the two that pass are the behaviours that did not change), **18 of 18 deliberate breaks of the code each turn a test red**, `npm test`: 1,795 pass. **Not done, said plainly:** (1) **the 1,000-row cap, probable and NOT MEASURED:** `readGhosts_` calls `db.rpc` bare and PostgREST caps a function that returns a set exactly as it caps a table (the note above `rpcAll` in `api/_lib/supabase.js`; paging it re-runs the function for every page), so a viewer whose teams hold more than 1,000 departed customers in all (a manager, a GMO or an admin, probably) receives the first 1,000 and loses the rest without a word: the thing the owner ordered against; the cure is a design (a function that answers one value under a new name, or the question asked a few teams at a time), not a patch; (2) **how often it runs:** every server instance asks for every team set it serves once every 90 seconds of use and the statement runs on until the role's limit even when the app has stopped waiting; a good list kept ten minutes instead of ninety seconds is 6.7 times fewer runs for a list up to ten minutes older after the morning upload, and it was not pulled for want of evidence that it is needed once RUN-ME-075 is in; (3) the region (section 8); (4) a kept check and pins for RUN-ME-075 (its header says each candidate asks the `(ref, day)` index: it does not); (5) nothing live seen; (6) **a correction of #754: counted in pages the cheaper function reads the SAME pages as the first (CPU only, 1.4 to 1.8 times quicker warm), so RUN-ME-075 alone is not expected to take the timeouts away and "no fall after it" does not clear the function; the partial index on the current rows that cuts the pages 6 times is measured (it costs +20% on inserting a deck day) and offered, not run (Part 114).**
- **#753 — no second demand notice while the last one is not cleared (20 Nov; handbook Part 113; `BUILD 2026-11-20-b`).** "Avoid duplicate demand notices - one cant create a second when the last one is not yet cleared" — the owner, 20 Nov, and, asked what is then allowed: "so one can create demand for the same ref no if the existing is cleared and the new one is 2dc+ or ds 11/12" (section 2). **Two questions, both must pass:** (1) the customer is at 2 DC and above or at D.S 11-12 (`legalEligible_`, Part 111, unchanged); (2) no earlier notice stands in the way: the customer's LAST notice (any date, from the app or from the register's upload) is not cleared (`legalOpenNotice_`). **"Cleared"** is the register's own test (`noticeCleared_`: owes nothing, or off the deck: the State column's "Amemaliza / Cleared") applied to the time since the notice: the customer owes nothing today, or on a day a defaulters deck was uploaded after the notice (`defaulterDeckDays`, the calendar the dashboard already reads, `deck_totals_days`) was off that day's current deck or owed nothing on it. So a customer served in September who cleared in October and is in default again may be served again; one who stood on every deck since may not. **What changed:** `legalPreview` answers `openNotice` (the notice in the way, or null); `addDemandNotice` refuses with Swahili and English words that name the notice (the guard rides in the same round of reads as the number, the teams, the brand and the officer's phone); the drawer names it, withholds the button and offers that notice's reprint; and the Issue button is off from the press until the server has answered (the officer pressing Issue again each time the print did not appear is how one customer got six letters in a day). Reprints are never gated. **Cost:** nothing for a customer with no notice (the usual case: no read added); for one who has a notice, a read of that customer's own deck rows since it (the `(ref, day)` index) and the cached calendar; creation is an officer's act, a few a day; nothing on `/api/upload` or `/api/call`; `test/speed.test.mjs` unchanged. **Checked:** a dedicated test (no notice; a notice in the way on the same day, a week later, by another officer and with no preview; the reprint; another customer; a notice that came from the register's upload; owing nothing, with the tab's state and the guard asked of the same row) and a second (stood on every deck since; off one deck; the same absence on a day with no deck; no calendar, empty or the table missing; a settled row; the DC rule asked first and on its own; D.S 11-12; today's file not yet in); the three older tests that issued two notices to one customer rewritten to a customer with no notice; the drawer's pins; **22 of 22 deliberate breaks of the code and the drawer each turn a test red**; `npm test`: 1,788 pass. **Mistakes of mine (Part 113):** the first build read "cleared" as today's reading alone, which would have barred every customer who cleared and fell back into default from ever being served again (the owner's second message said what is allowed); two breaks were not caught at first (the drawer pin's slice ran to the end of EVERY module, so another module's text satisfied it, a weakness older than this change; and the fake database answers an unknown table with empty rows, so the no-calendar path was never taken until `missingTables` was used). **Not done, said plainly:** a notice is judged by the deck calendar (a day uploaded but never built reads as "did not clear", the safe side; a team missing from one day's file reads as cleared that day, the whole-book rule); two officers issuing for one customer within the same second can still both pass (the guard reads before it writes; the button now stops the one officer); the pick list does not mark customers who have an open notice (that is a read per search); nothing seen on the live screen.
- **#750 — the duplicate demand notices: `db/RUN-ME-074` (pasted in the chat and RUN as DAY on 20 Nov), with its kept check and pins (handbook Part 112).** "SQL to delete duplicate demand notices by ref no" — the owner, 20 Nov. **A notice's ref is the customer's loan reference** (`demand_notices.ref`); its own number, the Kumb.Na. (`notice_id`), is unique by an index, so the repeats are several notices for one ref. Two ways they arise are in the code: the officer pressing Issue again because the print did not appear (`legalPreview`'s own comment: "the register showed one customer served six times on one day"; the drawer now warns, and a second letter is a decision), and a register line beside an app-made notice for the same customer. The owner's data does not say which made these. **What the file does,** in ONE statement (all of it or none): groups the notices by ref (blanks trimmed, any case; a blank or null ref is never touched), keeps ONE in each group, **the one issued first** (earliest notice date, then earliest issue time, then the one that carries a Kumb.Na.), copies every other notice WHOLE (jsonb, its stored letter included) to `demand_notices_removed` (private) and deletes it. **The word on its CHANGE line:** `DAY` (what was sent) removes only a repeat served on the SAME day as an earlier notice of that ref; `REF` removes every later notice of the ref, whatever its day. Any other word removes and copies nothing. **Why DAY was sent:** the code says a customer can be served again on a later day (`The same person can be served more than once; the reference is what must stay unique`, `db/migrations/2026-07-26-demand-notice-id.sql`), and the Legal screens disagree on which notice speaks for a customer across days (the tab's tiles read the EARLIEST, `noticeTiles_`; the dashboard card and the lawyers' board the LATEST, `legalFold_`), so which one stands is a decision, not a cleanup. The undo (the last two comment lines) puts back everything the file removed. Nothing else is touched: no payment, no recoveries ledger (a same-day repeat shares its (ref, day) with the notice that stands, so every ledger key still has a notice behind it), no deck. **THE OWNER'S ANSWER (as DAY): `DAY | DONE | 1127 | 60 | 93 | 1034 | 48`** — 1,127 notices carried a ref, **60 refs had a same-day repeat, 93 repeats were removed, 1,034 notices are left, and 48 refs were served on more than one day** (their notices on other days were left, as DAY is built to). **Checked** (`tools/db-checks/duplicate-notices.mjs`, run by hand on a PostgreSQL made from the whole of `db/schema.sql` and the two real migrations, 1 minute 20 seconds): the notices left equal a model written again in both modes and when DAY is followed by REF; the answer's numbers equal the model's; every removed notice is in the copy whole with the id of the one that stands; a second run removes nothing and writes nothing; the undo restores the table byte for byte after DAY, after REF and after both; a refused word removes nothing; the ledger is untouched; **the text cut at 150 places (every line and every 53rd character): 96 refused with an error, 38 ran without touching a notice, 16 ran to the end, no partial change**; the same text indented answers the same; **112,000 notices (a hundred times the live book's 1,127) answered as the model says, 30,000 removed, in 686 ms**; **21 of 21 deliberate breaks of the file each fail a check.** `test/duplicate-notices.test.mjs` (9 pins; **41 of 41 breaks of the pins caught**, after one pin was found loose by that run: a copy narrowed by a `where false` got past it). `npm test`: 1,786 pass. **Cost:** none to the system: words and a SQL file, no code that runs for a user, no `BUILD` change; one pass over about 1,100 rows. **Mistakes of mine, in Part 112:** `db/RUN-ME-074` entered the repository inside #749's commit (a `git add -A` took it in) before its check and pins existed, said in that PR's body; and the check first claimed "a hundred times the live book" on a book of 12,000 notices, about ten times, found when its numbers were read back for this entry (the book is now 112,000). **Not done, said plainly:** **REF is not run** (it removes at least 48 more notices, the first of each such ref standing: the owner's to say, section 8); a removed repeat's Kumb.Na. (`-2`, `-3` of a day) can be given again by `nextNoticeId`, which counts from the highest suffix left, and the undo then skips that one notice (`on conflict do nothing`) instead of failing; nothing was done about repeats in the other registers (complaints, restructures, deductions), which the uploads already replace by their own keys; nothing was seen on the live book beyond the owner's answer.
- **#749 — a demand notice's rule of 2 DC does not apply at D.S 11-12 (20 Nov; handbook Part 111).** "The rule of 2 installments shouldnt apply for DS 11-12 because there cant be two there" — the owner, 20 Nov (section 2). **The rule that was there** is the owner's own (the comment of `LEGAL_MIN_DC`: "a demand notice cant be generated for an expected/defaulter not from 2 DC and above"): the deck's D.C must be at least 2, asked by the preview (`eligible`), by the pick list (the line red below it) and by the guard in `addDemandNotice`. **At D.S 11-12** (D.S is paid-of-target: eleven of the twelve installments due are paid) only the LAST installment can be missing, so D.C is at most 1 and such a customer could never be served, however long the finished loan stood unpaid. **Now one function decides** (`legalEligible_`, `api/_lib/portal-core.js`): the rule is waived when D.S reads 11-12 (read by the phone's own reader `dsParts`, "11-12" or "11/12", spaces aside; the D.C is not asked: the rule is not theirs), else D.C at least 2 exactly as before. The preview says `eligible` and `dcWaived`; the pick list carries `eligible` on every line and the drawer no longer decides the rule itself (`var low = !r.eligible`); the guard refuses with the old words. The drawer says why the button is there at DC 1. **Not changed, said plainly:** the rule for every other D.S (a customer at 10-11 can still reach two, so the rule stands for them); and **the fine's own rule** (`legalFine`: one missed payment on a loan that has not expired yet owes no fine) is a different rule: a D.S 11-12 customer served before the loan has expired reads a fine of 0. **Cost:** none: no read added anywhere, a regex on one string per row; nothing on `/api/upload` or `/api/call` (`dsParts` is imported; `call-core.js` is unchanged); `test/speed.test.mjs` unchanged; `BUILD 2026-11-20-a` (the Legal module changed). **Checked:** one new test through the real dispatch (D.S 11-12 at DC 1 is served and the letter issued; '11/12', spaces and a deck row with no D.C likewise; 10-11, 9-10, 8-9, 11-11, 12-12, 1-12, 11-13, an empty D.S, 'n/a' and no D.S at all are refused as before; 9-12 and 10-12 as before; the pick list per line) and the drawer's pin; **13 of 13 deliberate breaks of the code and the drawer each turn a test red**; `npm test`: 1,777 pass. **Not done, said plainly:** nothing seen on the live screen; whether the fine should start earlier for D.S 11-12 is not asked (section 8, offered).
- **#748 — the first of the 13 replies, relayed bare (17 Nov, night): a one-row accounts script that finds the person by the name the registry carries now (handbook Part 110, addendum 4; docs only).** The owner pasted, with no words, one staff's reply to the message for the WhatsApp group of #746: an account number, a name spelt in full, and a zone typed with a letter missing. **Read as** the reply of the one person from that zone on the 072 list (072 prints zone, team and the registry's name, no staff ID; the reply's name carries both words of the registry's, the first spelt with a variant, and a surname the registry does not hold). By the owner's order of 17 Nov ("the provided is latest", Part 101) **the name is replaced and the account filled; the bank was NOT given, so it is left as it is and the answer says "not given"** (nothing is guessed from the number: Part 107). **Why a new script:** the by-name accounts template (D, #742) is about 25,000 characters stripped and every block the owner pastes stays under 10,000 (section 7); the update template needs a staff ID the list does not print. **The script:** one statement, 6,693 characters for one row (thirteen rows are 7,596, so the other twelve replies are added to the same text as they come), flush-left, plain ASCII, an END line, the undo at the foot. Each row is a person found by the name the registry carries now (or the reply's, so a second run still finds them), not Inactive, in the zone and team the list printed; the name goes in upper-case with blanks run together, the bank and the account are written only when the row has them (an account of digits, blanks and dashes is written as digits); **never the role, zone or status, never an emptied box**; it refuses, changing nothing, with the reason on the row: NOT FOUND (the records that carry the old name elsewhere are listed), MORE THAN ONE (both IDs), ANOTHER RECORD ALREADY CARRIES THE NEW NAME (Inactive ones too: a script never makes two records of one name), LISTED TWICE; the old row is copied first, once for each label (`staff_registry_update_before`, the update template's table, the same DDL), and a second run changes nothing. **Checked** on a PostgreSQL made from the whole of `db/schema.sql`: 33 checks (every box written and every box that must not change, the answer's words, a second run, a second run of an EDITED text keeping the first copy, the undo, the states of the registry, four rows in one text), **320 cut points: no partial change**, every line indented a little more than the one above (the same answer), 72 to 84 ms on 5,005 staff, and **23 of 23 deliberate breaks of the text each caught by a failing check**. **Mistakes of mine, in Part 110, addendum 4:** the first break run caught nothing in the scenarios that built their own text (they never sent the broken copy), so it was started again after the harness was fixed; four scenarios were added after reading what the first breaks left uncaught. **Cost:** none: words only, no code, no `BUILD` bump, nothing on `/api/upload` or `/api/call`. **Not done, said plainly:** nothing run on the live book from here, so its answer is not in; the bank is not known; the other 12 replies have not come; the filled script and its engine carry a name and an account and are not committed.
- **Outside the repository, 17 Nov — the owner's second internal audit report, cut to one page (a Word file the owner holds).** "Remove the the 2nd neglegance, just add the two loans ... into established totals ... as loass not mentioning wether fraud or neglegance - Single page is enogh then" — the owner, with the report and the external audit report attached (the names and amounts are in the owner's files, not here). Done on the owner's own file, edited in place (the letterhead and the auditor's signature kept): the second negligence section and the section on policy violations are gone; the two further loans are added, at their balances, to the total the first report established (and the external review confirmed) as a loss, with the sum shown; **no loan is called fraud or negligence anywhere in the file**, its title line and headings included; one page, checked in a render and by the validator. **Left out, and said to the owner:** the paragraph that named the three company SIM lines, and the reference to the policy items of the first report's Section 10 (that section is headed negligence). The old first-page thumbnail the file carried, which still showed the old title, was taken out of the package. Nothing of the report is in the repository: no name, no number, no file.
- **#746 — the answers of RUN-ME-072 and RUN-ME-073 run again (17 Nov, evening): the Customer Service accounts worked; 13 staff still have no account; one roster name still does not link (handbook Part 110, addendum 3; docs only).** The owner pasted the two answers with no words (steps 4 and 5 of the list of #745; step 3, the one-person script A or B, has no answer yet). **072 (November, Approved):** exactly the **13** people the registry holds no account for, **TZS 1,950,000** (Mwanza 5, Mara 3, and Dar North, North, North Coast, South West and Western one each; one request each; two of the Mara officers have no team in the registry either). No Customer Service name, no request with no staff ID and no staff ID missing from the registry is left on it, so the link (C2), the roster (E) and the accounts (D) did what they were built for. It was 14 people and TZS 2,150,000 before; the one that went is a South West request of TZS 200,000 (that zone goes 2 to 1, the others are unchanged). **073:** **14 of the 15 roster names are found by the request tab**, each with a real account, and **none has a request filed with no staff ID**; **two of the 14 have no bank name** (the two D added with none); **one still says NO**: the roster holds two names and the registry's record in the Customer Service department three, with the surname one letter shorter, so no staff ID is found for a request she files until the two match (the Advance and Leave tabs both match the roster this way). **Handed over in the chat, not committed (names):** the message for the WhatsApp group, in Swahili, listing the 13 by zone, team and name and asking each for the bank, the account number and the name on the account (the two Mara officers also for their team); and the question of which spelling is hers (the registry's: type it in Settings, Call agents; another: one script renames the registry record, not built until she says). **Cost:** none: words only, no code, no `BUILD` bump, nothing on `/api/upload` or `/api/call`. **Not done, said plainly:** nothing run from here; the 13 accounts, the two banks and the spelling are the staff's and the owner's to give; A or B has no answer; the group's replies are filled into the accounts script (D, by name) when they come.
- **#745 — the owner's answers of 17 Nov, evening: 060 and 066 for 1 to 9 Nov FINISHED; the one-person staff script reached the editor cut at 20,000 characters and nothing ran; two small scripts instead (handbook Part 108, addendum 3, and Part 110, addendum 2; docs only).** The owner pasted three answers and a failure, with no words. **060 for 1 to 9 Nov: FINISHED** (`13007 | 13007 | 13007 | 0 | 0`: 13,007 did not fit, one reading fitted all of them, all 13,007 put right, 0 still to do, 0 left alone). Over the window 060 put right 213,007 rows in five working runs (four of 50,000 and this one), and 071 had put right the other 64 of the 213,071 first counted. **066 for 1 to 9 Nov: FINISHED** (its fifth run): 286,575 deck rows in the window, **26 uploads found swapped and 2 left alone** (the initial decks of 6 and 7 Nov: 060 put right none of their 20,891 rows and none shows the exchange: they were read right in the first place), **4,955 changed by this run, 0 still to do, 0 left alone for want of 060, 0 impossible readings**, 7,779 rows left alone in the two read-right uploads (correct as stored). Ill go for to long bro" -- the owner, under the first answer of the day-by-day form (3 days in its 10-second clock, 13 to 15 Nov, nothing to repair there, 104 days to do: 35 runs). **Why:** a day is 10,600 rows, and on the live table they do not lie together, so a day is thousands of pages fetched from disk; progress per team in `defaulter_last_known_repair_teams` (with the window it was done for: a wider window redoes a team done for a narrower one); the clock (`lk_seconds` 15) read after each team; **the answer says `seconds_this_run`**; refuses with a plain message when the index is missing. `enable_bitmapscan = off` and `(s.team || '') = v_team` are what keep the look to a row or two (the first draft read 54 pages per loan number: a bitmap scan collected all 53 rows of each), both pinned and both broken on purpose. **Measured in PAGES on a 2.27-million-row book with EVERY ROW IN A RANDOM PLACE:** the whole window touches 151,674 table pages for 42,123 loan numbers (3.6 each; - **#737 — RUN-ME-071 timed out on the live book; it now runs one day at a time, with the clock on (handbook Part 106). THAT SECOND FORM COST ABOUT 3 SECONDS A DAY LIVE and is replaced by #739 (team by team).** "071 / Error: SQL query ran into an upstream timeout" -- the owner, 17 Nov, on the whole-range form of #735. **Why:** that form asked for the whole range in ONE statement (a pass over the deck table: 172,196 pages touched on a 2.27-million-row test book of 84,000 pages); "2 seconds" was a measurement of a cache and of a layout (rows written customer after customer), not a bound, and the editor gave up before the file's own 50-second ceiling ("upstream timeout", not "statement timeout"). **Whether it changed anything is not known:** the owner was given a read-only check (rows in `defaulter_last_known_repair_log`, 0 or no table = rolled back; whether a query that names it is still running) and its answer is not in (section 8). **The file now:** a `do` block, one day at a time, NEWEST day first (the customers cleared lately), a clock (`lk_seconds` 10: a run always finishes the day it is on and starts another only while the clock allows), every finished day written down (`defaulter_last_known_repair_days`, primary key the day, RLS on), so the owner runs THE SAME TEXT AGAIN until `what_next` says `FINISHED`; per day: the keys of the next deck day spare the customers who are on it, the few hundred who are not get one `limit 1` look in the `(ref, snapshot_date)` index (`left join lateral`, never a hash join over the table), then RUN-ME-060's rule word for word (pinned against 060's text) and RUN-ME-055's choice of the last-known row (pinned); `statement_timeout` 30 s (the database cuts a stuck script off by itself, nothing changed), `plan_cache_mode = force_custom_plan`, `enable_seqscan = off`; `lk_apply 'no'` counts and writes nothing; the undo at the foot restores the dates and clears the days. **Cost, in pages (they do not depend on the cache), on a 2.27-million-row book laid out day after day:** a one-day run touches 3,300 to 5,900 pages (10,400 when it writes 230 rows), **the same on half the book**, 74,988 with the next-deck test taken out, 172,196 for the first form; its whole-window numbers equal the first form's (31,268 last-known rows, 19,685 repaired-able). Nothing on `/api/upload` or `/api/call`; `BUILD`/`CALL_BUILD` unchanged. **Checked:** `tools/db-checks/last-known-repair.mjs` rewritten (customers who left and came back, a day a whole team is missing, Sundays with no deck, a database made slow on purpose, a run cut off by the database's own limit, the pages of a run on a day-by-day book; **22 of 22 deliberate breaks change the outcome**, plus the clock and the next-deck test) and `test/last-known-repair.test.mjs` (8 pins; **71 of 71 breaks of the pins and the check caught**, after 5 were weak at first). **Mistakes of mine (Part 106):** the first form was timed on a cached book and the check compared answers, never pages; in the new check a break was not caught because the count ran after the apply had done every day, the measured day was a quiet one, the half book was made with a plain `vacuum`; five pins matched a comment of the check. **Not done, said plainly:** nothing run on the live book; the live seconds a day, and so the number of runs, are not known; a day re-uploaded after it was written down needs its row deleted from the days table to be looked at again; a customer's earlier rows are not repaired (Part 104).

  **What it left: 252 imported notices whose date is not their number's day, none after the upload day. 251 of them follow ONE rule: the stored date is the number's day with day and month exchanged, less one day (190), or, where the day equals the month, the number's day less one (61: all eight printed are 10/10/2026 stored 9 Oct); 1 is something else. The stamp's day is the stored date on 243 of the 252 and the number's day on none. 0 of the 566 notices that match their number and 0 of the 248 made in the app show it.**
  The page of 12 Oct replayed in nine time zones (East Africa, UTC, London, New York, Los Angeles, Dubai, Kolkata, Nairobi, Sydney) never makes the early day (an exact exchange everywhere, never a lost day): the cause is upstream of the page (the register sheet's cells, or a route that is not the page). **Not found; a chat does not look again in the importer or the page for it** (Part 98 and 100).
  **`db/RUN-ME-070` changes a notice only when all four hold:** (1) it is imported and its date is not its number's day (the number a day that exists, not after the upload day); (2) **its upload is PROVEN**: RUN-ME-068 put right three or more notices of that very upload because they were dated after it (068's log; 070 writes to logs of its own, so nothing is proved by itself); (3) **the stored date is EXACTLY the shape** (the exchange less one day; or day = month, less one day; never the exact exchange, never a day late, never a number with a day over twelve); (4) no earlier run changed it.
  The notice date goes to the number's day; the stamp moves only if its East Africa day is the same wrong day (same time of day); the ledger rows (RUN-ME-041a, keyed by notice date) move with it unless another notice of the loan carries the old or the new date (068 had no such guard) or the payment is already at the new date; a payment the day before the corrected date is counted and never deleted. Two logs of its own, the undo at the foot, a second run changes nothing.
  **The answer measures what it does not touch:** every imported notice by what its number's day can be and whether it now equals it (lines 11 to 14); the "one day early" left, by the same classes (**line 16: a day over twelve is the line that says the shift is more than an exchange**); and the notices whose date is right but whose stamp is on another day (line 7).
  **Cost:** about 1,100 rows read; 2.2 s on a book a hundred times the live one; nothing on `/api/upload` or `/api/call`; no `BUILD` change. **Checked:** `tools/db-checks/notice-shift-repair.mjs` on the real schema, the real 041a migration and THE REAL 068 RUN FIRST, 297 notices (four uploads with 12, 2, 0 and exactly 3 proofs): every row of both tables, both logs and every printed line equals a model written again, a second run changes nothing, the undo is byte for byte, a book without 068's log or the ledger is refused, a book with no proof says so; **57 of 57 deliberate breaks change the outcome**. `test/notice-shift-repair.test.mjs` (11 tests, **56 of 56 breaks of the pins caught**). **Three mistakes of mine the check found (Part 100):** the fixture's kinds were never attached to the rows the model read back (the assertion failed, as it is for); eight breaks passed unseen for want of a fixture row (one was an equivalent mutant); a "plain day early" row had day = month and was repaired.
  **The other answers of the same paste:** **060 for 10 to 15 Nov is FINISHED** (third run: 10,328 repaired, 0 left; 110,328 rows in all). **066 for 10 to 15 Nov, first run:** 14 uploads swapped, 3 left alone (the initial decks of 10, 14 and 15 Nov, read right in the first place: their 11,985 "another reading" rows are dates like 3/6, right as stored), 53,434 rows safe, **50,000 changed, 3,434 still to do**. **063 failed in the editor** ("upstream timeout": it reads the decks table end to end): skipped, nothing written. 058 and 059 not yet run ("I have not proceeded to 6 - 058 yet").
  **Not done, said plainly:** nothing run on the live book; if some of the 61 have a day over twelve they are NOT repaired (line 16 counts them); the one "further" notice is left; the stamps of right-dated notices are counted, not repaired; **other registers imported through the same route on 12 Oct (deductions, complaints, restructures, advances) may carry the same shape and none was looked at**; the five-row deduction case; the last-known deck rows and the Expected sheet's dates (Part 91).

The names are in the owner's messages and in no file of the repository.** The owner posted the 49 names in the WhatsApp group; the replies came back as forms and short messages and were relayed here. A filled copy of `db/TEMPLATE-staff-changes-from-gmo.sql` (PLACE rows only, each person asked for by the REGISTRY'S OWN spelling as the list printed it: a name a form spells longer is never re-spelt; **no row has a `zone_else`, so a name that is not found answers NOT ADDED and cannot be created by mistake**) went to the owner in the chat.   **Read from the messages, and said to the owner:** (1) one reply (a field officer, a CRDB account, team Temeke) was filed on the only Dar Center name on the list that had no team, spelt in a Swahili variant: taken to be that person and flagged (the sender manages other teams); (2) one reply gave an account only (no team, no bank name): the account was filed, **the team is still missing**; (3) **a GMO's form carries a NIDA of 22 digits whose date part is not the birth date the form gives: the NIDA was NOT written** (the birth date is the form's); ask for it again; (4) one form gives no role: the role stays as filed, and a brother with a number and no name is not filed; (5) **one CRDB account has 11 digits** (CRDB's are usually 13 characters): written as given, flagged; (6) two accounts have letters in the middle: stored as the text given, as the template does. E-mail and marital status are not stored (no box); NSSF and TIN written nil, on process, hana or -- are left empty. **39 of the 49 names have not answered.** No code or rule changed.

- **#730 — the owner's answer to RUN-ME-067 read (17 Nov), and `db/RUN-ME-068`, which repairs the notice and deduction dates that cannot be real (handbook Part 98, pasted in full in the chat).** **067's answer:** of 1,094 notices **0** carry their number's day exchanged and **280 imported ones match neither**; all **12** dated later than today are among them, and **every one of the twelve is the number's day with day and month exchanged AND a day early**
  (number 12 Oct → stored 9 Dec; 12 Jun → 5 Dec; ten of the twelve carry the same day in the stamp); all **248** made in the app agree with their number. **Seven** deductions are out of order (two cases): the two rows discovered after the submission have ONE reading that fits (12 Jun 2026); **the five rows discovered before the money was received do not**: exchanging the discovery, the received date or both puts them in order,
  so 067's "7 of 7 fixable" was true and not proof (said in Part 98; 067's header was incomplete). **Where the extra day comes from was looked for and NOT found:** the importer of 12 Oct is today's; the page of that day formats dates with the browser's local getters; replayed in a real Chromium set to `Africa/Dar_es_Salaam`, a day-first CSV gives an exact exchange and Excel date cells arrive exact: nothing runnable makes "exchanged and a day early"
  (Safari and Firefox not tried): a chat does not look again for it in the code, and the optional question to the owner is in section 8. **What 068 does:** a notice (imported, upload date set) is changed ONLY WHEN (1) its date is later than the day it was uploaded (its own `upload_date`), (2) its number carries a real day not later than that, (3) the stored date is that day exchanged, exactly or a day early:
  `notice_date` goes back to the number's day; its stamp moves with it only if the stamp's own day is also after the upload day and the same exchange (same time of day, East Africa); its recoveries in `notice_payment_ledger` follow the date (never onto a row already there; every move in `notice_ledger_date_move_log`). A deduction's discovery date is changed ONLY WHEN it is later than the submission day,
  the submission day reads one way, and ONE reading fits (not after the submission, not before the money under either reading of it). **Left as they are, counted and DESCRIBED in the answer (how far, which way, the eight commonest differences, eight examples):** every other imported notice that differs from its number (at most 268; **not understood, so not touched**), the five-row deduction case (it prints which readings fit). No name, number or amount is printed.
  Three small log tables (not readable through the public API), a row in a log is never changed again (safe to run again; an undone row stays undone), the undo at the foot, three statements. **Cost:** about 1,200 rows; 1.9 s on a book a hundred times the live one (5,000 to repair, a 40,000-row ledger); nothing on `/api/upload` or `/api/call`; no `BUILD` change.
  **Checked** on a PostgreSQL from the real schema, the two real migrations and the real RUN-ME-041a (the three logs made from the file's own DDL): every row of three tables and three logs and every printed line equals a model written again; a second run changes nothing; the undo puts every table back byte for byte; a book without the ledger is refused; **47 of 47 deliberate breaks change the outcome**;
  `test/notice-date-repair.test.mjs` (12 tests, **46 of 46 breaks of the pins caught**). **Three mistakes of mine the check found (Part 98):** the first undo would have failed on a real book (a ledger row already at the new date: now logged and restored exactly), a fixture's "earlier run" rows were reversed, a read-back folded two rows into one. **Not done, said plainly:** nothing run on the live book; the notices not dated after their upload day;
  the five-row deduction case; the extra day unexplained (no importer or page changed for it); a stamp wrong while its notice date is right; the Expected sheet's dates and the decks' `due_date`.
