AWS steps are skipped because their settings were never filled in. Vercel still deploys `main` by
itself, through its own link to GitHub, with no gap. Our own server restarts; measured twice
(section 5): once nothing was refused, once one request of eighty-four was not answered.

**People with access to the repository:** `samaritantechs` (the owner, admin) and `monkyaro`
(Demit, write).

## 5. In flight: moving to our own server — STOPPED, the two sites are two books

The order is in section 2 (11 Nov). Our own server is `https://recovery-app.sync-book.net`: one
machine, one Node process (`server.js`) behind Caddy, kept in step with `main` by the deploy
workflow. Vercel is `https://hope-pmo-v2-git-main-samaritantechs.vercel.app`, the address every
installed app opens today.

**Nobody has been moved, and nobody may be until the two sites read one database.** Each site
says which book it reads (`book` in `/api/app-version`, handbook Part 43). On 11 Nov they
answered:

| Site | `book` | Which database |
|---|---|---|
| Vercel | `79e363955e75` | the Supabase project: the book every installed app has worked on since August |
| Our own server | `9372d032c6ca` | `127.0.0.1:8000`: a database on the server's own machine, holding a **copy** |

Work done on our own server stays in the copy: it is on no report, on no other phone and in no
commission, and neither site says so. **Do no real work at the new address** until this is settled.

**It happened on the night of 11 Nov** (handbook Part 51). The owner edited Teams & Staff at the
new address and nothing showed at the old one. A script was handed over that carries the edits
into the real book, and a staff script of two rows to run after it; **both were run on 12 Nov**
(section 4), so those edits are in the real book now. Anything else done at the new address is
still only in the copy. A chat that sees an address in the owner's message reads WHICH site it
is before anything else, and says so in its first line if it is the new one.

**What has to be decided — by the owner, with Demit — is which database is the book:**

1. **Supabase stays the book** (the small step, and the one recommended). In `/etc/hope-pmo.env`
   on the server, `SUPABASE_URL` and `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY` take the values Vercel has, and
   the service is restarted. The two sites are then one system and the move can go ahead at once.
2. **The database on our own machine becomes the book.** That moves the database, not an
   address: `docs/DEPLOY.md` section 7 lists what it takes.

**How a chat knows it is settled:** open the two addresses below. The same `book` on both is one
system. Check it first, every time, before touching the app's address.

    https://recovery-app.sync-book.net/api/app-version
    https://hope-pmo-v2-git-main-samaritantechs.vercel.app/api/app-version

**Then, and only then — Android app 20.** It opens our own server. If that cannot be opened (the
server is down, or the handset is too old to trust its certificate) the app opens Vercel by
itself, and only while Vercel answers the same `book`. The update check asks the reserve too, so
a handset can always be reached with a newer app. Nothing of this is built yet: which way the
reserve points depends on the decision above.

**What the analysis of 11 Nov added (handbook Part 45).** Three readings of the code finished
(the Android app; browsers and the systems that hold the old address; uploads and feeders). Five
did not: the account's usage limit cut the run. What they changed in the plan:

- **Never a redirect at Vercel or Caddy.** The installed app refuses any host but its own and
  hands the new address to the phone's browser: no call-log sync, an unregistered device. The
  forwarding is done inside the page: a browser is sent on (path, query and hash kept); a handset
  is never redirected.
- **App 20 goes out before any handset is moved.** A version-19 handset that has saved the new
  address has no way back if that server cannot be opened; its update check asks only the saved
  address. Handsets then move a team at a time, never all on one deploy.
- **Company phones with the lock app keep the Vercel address for life** (it is written once, at
  enrolment). Vercel must go on answering `/api/device` from the book the office uses, or issued
  phones lock themselves after 14 days. This is why "Supabase stays the book" is the way
  recommended: the other way needs Vercel pointed at the server's database in the same minute.
- **Anything that feeds data in automatically goes through `POST /api/upload`**, never straight
  into the tables: the upload also stamps the batch, rebuilds the officers' list, builds the
  totals and tells the handsets. `docs/DEPLOY.md` section 7 has the request.
- **Ours to build next, in this order:** the in-page forwarding (armed only when both sites read
  one book); app 20; the upload page naming the address and book it writes to and resending a
  slice after a 502 or 503; `server.js` refusing to forward a request to itself; the deploy check
  asking the running process for its version.
- **To confirm, then fix:** the sweep after an upload keeps the two newest uploads of a day across
  all teams, so a day sent as three files may lose the first.

**Also found on 11 Nov:**

- Our own server is up, serves the same `BUILD` and `CALL_BUILD` as Vercel, compresses, and
  answers "not changed". A deploy restarted it while it was asked for its health 484 times in five
  minutes: every one answered, none slower than a second. That was with no traffic. Under load
  the service takes no new connection until the requests in flight finish, which during an upload
  slice can be most of a minute. Measured again at the #672 deploy (11 Nov, 20:37 EAT): asked for
  its version 84 times across the restart, it left ONE unanswered (the status was not captured;
  the restart step itself took three seconds). The gap is real, and small.
- Its certificate is from Let's Encrypt. **Android 6 and 7.0 do not trust it** (that authority
  joined Android's trusted list in 7.1.1); Vercel's they do trust. A handset that old cannot open
  our own server at all. Not tried on such a phone: it is read off the certificate.

**For the web there is nothing to build**, once the books are one. People open the new address.
Each browser signs in once there (a browser keeps its access code per address) and allows
notifications again. The owner sets Settings → `APP_BASE_URL` to the new address, so the links in
e-mails lead to it.

## 6. Scheduled tasks

| Task | When | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| **HOPE PMO change check (8-hourly)** | 03:21, 11:21, 19:21 East Africa time | Fires into the current chat. Reports every change to the repository since the last check — commits, branches, pull requests, people with access, whether each merge deployed — and flags anything touching the protected paths. Report only: it never changes, reverts, merges or deploys. Its full instructions are stored in the task. |
| **HOPE PMO change report email** | when the check fires it | Sends the check's report to the owner's inbox. Runs in a session of its own. |
| **Historic sales upload reminder (Audit report total PAR)** | Mondays 07:52 East Africa time | Reminds the owner that the whole-portfolio PAR on the Audit report waits on the 2024 to July 2026 sales being uploaded. Runs in a session of its own. |

**The check dies with the chat it fires into.** A new chat lists the scheduled tasks; if the check
points at an older chat, it creates the same task on itself (copy the stored instructions, add its
own session to the "from this chat" rule, keep the last "Checked up to" commit as the starting
point), disables the old one, and tells the owner. Disable, never delete: the history stays.

## 7. How the work is done here

- **One branch per chat.** After each squash merge the branch is restarted from `main`:
  `git fetch origin main && git checkout -B <branch> origin/main && git push --force-with-lease -u origin <branch>`.
- **A pull request is opened ready, and merged as soon as `npm test` is green** (section 2). The
  title says what a person now sees, in the words of the others in `git log`. The body says what
  was asked, what changed, **what it cost the database**, and what was not done.
- **Every change also carries**: a Part in `docs/HANDOFF.md` with the owner's words; the `BUILD`
  bump (portal and modules) or the `CALL_BUILD` bump (call app); a tip in the `hints` table for
  anything a person would have to discover; and this file.
- **Screens are checked in a real browser** when a change is on a screen (Chromium and Playwright
  are already on the cloud machine; `tools/browser-checks/` holds the kept checks). A harness
  written in a chat's scratch folder is gone when the chat ends.
- **Long output ends chats.** Send it to a scratch file and read the part that is needed. Never
  print the whole of `public/app.html` (half a megabyte), a whole diff of it, or an image as text.
- **Start a new chat rather than stretch an old one.** Nothing is lost by it now.
- **A team of analysts can spend the account's five-hour allowance in ten minutes.** On 11 Nov
  eight of them did, and the chat could not answer the owner for over two hours. Keep a team
  small, give each member a narrow question, and deliver what is ready first.
- **`grep` calls `public/app.html` a binary file** and prints nothing useful: use `grep -a`.
- **A chat that has worked all day is at the limit.** The second team of 11 Nov (three reviewers,
  #669) lost two of its three to the same five-hour allowance within two minutes. Late in a long
  day, review alone: tests, a real browser, a real PostgreSQL. **It happened again on 15 Nov and
  cost more:** three reviewers on #705, started after a full day's work, used up the allowance
  without one finding, and the limit froze the chat itself — a change that was built, tested and
  green sat unmerged for seven and a half hours, the owner waiting, until "Please continue".
  A review that can stop the chat is never started while a finished change is waiting to be
  merged: merge first (section 2), then have it read if the allowance is there.
  **And a third time, the next morning (16 Nov).** One reader on the merged #705 came back with
  real findings and the chat answered. Then, with the corrected SQL owed to the owner, a
  five-agent workflow (two finders, three checkers) was started to look for the same fault
  elsewhere: the three checkers died on the allowance and the chat stopped from about 03:55 to
  08:30 EAT, the owner writing "Please continue" twice. **While the owner is waiting on
  something, no agent is started at all.** One reader at a time, only when nothing is owed, and
  its findings are checked by hand on the test database. A standing "use workflows" setting on
  the session does not change this: the allowance is the owner's, and it has frozen this chat
  three times.
- **A reader's finding is read against the rule that governs it before the owner is told it is
  real.** On 16 Nov a reader reported that a team missing from a day's current file was "read
  as recovered in full", with a reproduction, and `api/_lib/snapshots.js` seemed to back it
  ("a day can also arrive as SEVERAL files"). The owner was told the fault was real and a fix
  was built. The rule that governs the question is elsewhere, in the owner's words:
  `db/RUN-ME-032`'s header, `defaulterBook`, the upload's retirement note — "one file, all
  weekdays together, every time", "a team missing from it owes nothing on it, full stop". The
  reader could not have known; the chat could, and section 2 (14 Nov) already said to look. A
  reproduction shows what the code does. Only the Part and the owner's words say whether that
  is wrong. Grep the phrase of the finding ("missing from", "owes nothing", "cleared") across
  `db/*.sql` headers and `docs/HANDOFF.md` before answering.
- **A script for the SQL editor is timed on a book of the live book's size first.** One that takes
  seconds on the test machine is made lighter before it is handed over.
- **A document for the owner's management goes out whole.** On 11 Nov a report was sent with two
  lines still waiting on figures, and the answer was: "You cant provide report before including
  negligances bro". Say what is missing and what it waits on; send the document when nothing is.
- **A team's zone is its GMO's** (section 2, handbook Parts 50 and 52). A staff moved to a team
  with an empty ZONE cell is filed under the zone of that team's GMO, and its people read that
  zone. Only a team with no GMO, or whose GMO has no team with a zone, or whose GMO is named on
  teams of more than one zone, cannot receive anybody: ask the owner to name the GMO on it, or to
  type its ZONE.
- **A rule that has to choose is a guess.** "The zone most of the GMO's teams carry" was read by
  three reviewers and nine skeptics and none objected; the owner's own list had a GMO over four
  zones and the tie went to the alphabet. Where a rule would pick among answers, give none, say
  what was found, and let one typed cell settle it. And look for the OTHER places that pick: the
  review of #673 found the script still taking "the zone of the staff already on that team",
  which handed the tie's own answer to the next person sent there.
- **Read which site an address is before anything else.** On 11 Nov the owner was asked to name
  a GMO on a team, did it at the new address, and an evening of edits went into the copy. When a
  message carries `recovery-app.sync-book.net`, the first line of the answer says that it is the
  copy (while section 5 stands).
- **A chat can stop in the middle of a change without a word.** On the night of 11 Nov it did,
  for six hours, until the next scheduled check woke it; the change sat unpushed on the chat's
  machine. Push work in progress to the chat's own branch before a long step. On 12 Nov the
  machine itself restarted twice under three reviewers, each time losing the local test
  database (not the repository or the scratch folder): the second restart also emptied the
  account's usage credits, so the review that had failed once on a restart failed again on
  that, and #675 went in checked by hand instead (section 7). On 12 Nov the
  machine itself restarted under three reviewers: the pushed commit and the scratch folder were
  still there, the test database had to be started again, and the review was resumed from its
  run. Hand over what is ready before a review, when it is safe to run and to run again.
- **A long script for the SQL editor starts with `set local jit = off`.** The staff script took
  eight seconds on the test machine, all of it the database compiling the query; it ran in a
  fifth of a second. Time a script with the compiler on and off before believing either number.
- **A check is checked by breaking what it guards.** The first check that the portal and the
  script read a team alike passed, and went on passing with the rule broken: its random numbers
  were nearly always even, so half its cases were never drawn. Break the rule, see the check fail.
- **A list with names only is asked as MOVE.** A GMO who sends the whole staff of a team sends
  no phone and no account. Asked as PLACE or NEW, a name the registry spells another way (two
  letters in one word is enough) becomes a second person, and a second person cannot be taken
  back; asked as MOVE it comes back NOT FOUND with who carries each of the names, and the next
  script settles it. New staff are added from rows that carry a phone or an account.
- **Head-office people are filed by department and offered by role** (handbook Part 53). They
  have no team. The zone is the department as the registry already files it (`HQ - PMO`, `HQ -
  CREDIT`, `MANAGEMENT`: the owner's "pmo hq" is `HQ - PMO`), never the words of a message. The
  role is written as Roles & access spells it, never left to the script's default: the request
  tab offers a name only to the holders of its role. When a form gives no role, write the desk
  Teams & Staff names the person on, tell the owner which word was written for whom, and read
  the roles the answer lists for that zone.
- **A NEW that comes back ALREADY IN THE REGISTRY is asked again as PLACE.** The person is
  there, somewhere else: PLACE moves them to the team and fills only what is empty.
- **A staff found under a zone their team does not carry was on nobody's list.** When a GMO
  sends the whole staff of a team, expect the registry to hold them under an older zone. The
  MOVE that files them under their team's zone is what puts them on their team's code: say so
  to the owner, it is the reason the list was sent.
- **A NEW that comes back ALREADY IN THE REGISTRY is asked again as PLACE** — the person is
  there, and PLACE fills only what is empty. The same holds with no team at all: two
  head-office staff came back ALREADY IN THE REGISTRY under a bare department role, and a PLACE
  with no role or zone on the row touched neither, because every box was already filled.
- **A script never merges two staff records on a name alone.** "Look: a name like it is already
  in this zone" is the script noticing, not deciding; a staff script can switch one record to
  Inactive and move a form onto the other once the owner says they are the same person, and not
  before.
- **A name a list spells differently is asked for in the registry's own spelling.** When an
  answer shows the person (two names the same, the third a variant), the next script asks for
  them as the registry has them; nothing is re-spelt. Tell the owner which name was taken for
  which.
- **"Only find more rows, never fewer" is not the same promise as "cost nothing more."** A
  date-range filter dropped for fear of losing a row (Part 55) was dropped with no floor at
  all, not a wider one — fetching a whole book's history, in full, on every month a cached
  report is asked to look at, since the cache was keyed by month and the query no longer was.
  Widen a filter to stop it from losing something; dropping it outright is a different, much
  more expensive promise, and a cache keyed by a value a query no longer depends on buys
  nothing on the second ask.
- **A rescue keyed on the wrong thing can double what it was built to save from going
  missing.** The week's one-time catch for an undated customer (Part 54) was keyed by role and
  leader as well as the customer, assuming those stay put for the week; `days_elapsed` moves a
  customer's role on its own clock regardless of whether a date can be read, so the same
  customer crossed that boundary mid-week and was rescued once per leader instead of once for
  the week. Key a once-only rule on the one thing that is actually constant — here, the
  customer's own reference — never on a value that can legitimately change under it.
- **A `\uXXXX` typed into a command reaches the file as the character itself.** A no-break
  space, a line separator and a byte-order mark went into a script's pattern that way, invisible
  and waiting to be mangled by a paste. Write such a character with a doubled backslash, or build
  it in the script (`chr(92) + 'u00A0'`), and scan every file for characters outside plain text
  before it is committed or handed over. It happened again on 17 Nov (an ellipsis and a range of dashes written into `importers.js` by a python heredoc): the scan found it.
- **Two copies that agree can both be wrong.** The portal's rule and the script's read a dash in
  the GMO cell as a GMO, alike, so the check that compares them passed. A review by readers who
  had not written the change found it. Have a change read before it is merged, findings put to
  a second reader, when the allowance is there (three reviewers and nine skeptics on #671 cost
  about a quarter of an hour); when it is not, say that it was not read.
- **A zone has the name Teams & Staff files it under.** A GMO's word for a zone and the filed
  one can differ. Scripts and screens read the teams table; never type a zone from a message.
- **A database check is built from the book's own column types, never from what the query needs.** `db/RUN-ME-061` was run
  on a PostgreSQL, timed on 207,439 rows, compared line by line with a count made in JavaScript and broken on purpose, and still
  stopped at the owner's keyboard on the first run: its test table declared `upload_batch` as text and the real column is a
  uuid. Build a check's table from `db/schema.sql` (the columns, their types, their constraints), put in the awkward rows the
  real book has (a row with no batch), and keep the fixture's types pinned to the schema by a test that `npm test` runs
  (`test/deck-upload-audit.test.mjs`). A query the owner is about to paste into the SQL editor has run on the real types first.
- **A check whose clean answer would be read as "all okay" says, in the same breath, what a clean answer does not prove, and carries the
  test that does.** "Is any Disb date later than today?" (Part 91) can only ever see the swapped dates whose real day is the 12th: a
  zero would have read as "all the dates are right" while four fifths of them were not. The witness that sees the rest (the
  deck's day, the Disb date and the loan system's DAYS ELAPSED must agree) goes in the same file, and the verdict line is written so that
  it cannot say "all right" on the first test alone.
- **A new point of a browser check is seen RED on the code it was written against before it is believed, and the log of that run is read line by line, never by its totals** (handbook Part 131). The seventh point of the second reader's check held the server's answer for the week key `''`, but a list opened from the card asks for the card's OWN week, so the hold never applied: the point passed on the page as merged and on the fixed page alike, and the summary line "seven red" was written from the count of the reader's findings, not from the log. Read the merged-page log for every new point by name; a point that is not red there is not a point yet.
- **A check's fixture is counted before its breaks are believed.** The first fixture of `db/RUN-ME-066` asked for "loans whose three dates can all be
  misread": the chronic date is 14 days after the expiry, so there are none, and the special uploads built on them were empty or tiny while half the
  checks passed on nothing; later, two deliberate breaks were "not caught" because the fixture had no row of the kind each one needs. Build special rows
  from the days that exist and throw when there are none, count every kind of row a break needs and assert the count (`last-trans-repair.mjs` does), and
  count a broken file that fails to run as a failure of the check, never as a caught break.
- **A file the owner sends is checked with `tools/file-date-audit.mjs` before anything is said about it** (handbook Part 90).
  Its date columns, its order and a witness that is not the order rule: the loan system's own pattern for a deck, the Filters
  window for an approved report. The route a file took (Excel text, a CSV, real dates) changes what is stored whatever the
  file's order is: read what the book holds with `db/RUN-ME-061` before blaming the file. Ask the owner for ONE real file of
  each upload kind whose order has not been seen (section 8); a file's own words about itself are the best evidence there is.
- **Ask for the exports before writing a dig.** The loan system exports Approved (Processed) and
  Defaulters for one team and any dates, with balances. On 11 Nov an SQL dig that took 70 ms on a
  test book of the live book's size ended in "upstream timeout" in the SQL editor; the owner's
  two exports answered the same question in minutes ("Check from here no need of sql"). A dig is
  not proven until it has run on the live book, and a chat cannot run it there.
- **When the owner says two figures cannot be equal, find who each one is summed over before
  answering.** On 14 Nov the owner said the Expected Defaulters card could not equal the whole
  book's recovery, and was told the two were different formulas by design. They were: over the
  same customers. The card had been built on the Exp.Def Report's test for "in the file" without
  its test for "today is their day" (handbook Part 79). "By design" is an answer about the code;
  the owner's question was about the business, and the rule that answered it was already
  written three files away.
- **A corrected database function gets a new name.** A page that asks for the old name on a
  book where the new file has not been run would go on showing the old, wrong figure with
  nothing to say so. Under a new name it gets no answer, says "no answer yet" and names the file.
- **A mirror the tests read is checked against the real function on a PostgreSQL**
  (`tools/db-checks/expdf-cycle-recovery-parity.mjs`, as `gmo-zone-parity.mjs` does for the
  zone rule): random cases through both, fail on the first difference, refuse to pass when the
  hard cases were not drawn, and break the rule on purpose to see it fail.
- **A tab still reading "Running..." may already be dead.** `RUN-ME-054`'s first
  `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` (12 Nov) sat in the SQL editor reading "Running..." long after the
  server-side build had actually stopped -- the editor's own connection had dropped, Postgres had
  already rolled the build back, and the tab was just waiting on a reply that was never coming.
  It left an invalid index behind under the exact name the next attempt's `IF NOT EXISTS` would
  then silently protect. Check `pg_stat_progress_create_index` from a SECOND tab before trusting
  a first tab's spinner on any long `CONCURRENTLY` statement; zero rows there means nothing is
  really running, whatever the first tab says. `select indexrelid::regclass::text, indisvalid
  from pg_index where indexrelid::regclass::text = '<name>'` catches the leftover; `drop index
  concurrently if exists <name>` (alone, the same rule as `CREATE`) clears it before retrying.
- **A pin is checked by breaking what it guards: in every place the thing appears, and in the code, never the comment** (handbook Part 101). The first mutation run of the staff-update pins found one pin that looked at the first of eight places a phone is read, and two that a comment in the check's header could satisfy: three of 42 breaks passed. A pin that matches a header comment goes on passing after the code stops doing what the comment says, and a pin on "the first match" guards one place of N. Strip the comment before matching, count the places, and break each place on its own.
- **A statement that has to run in the SQL editor is time-boxed by design, and its cost is measured in PAGES** (handbook Part 106). The whole-range form of RUN-ME-071 was run on a PostgreSQL, timed (2 seconds), compared with a model and broken on purpose, and ran past the editor's patience on the live book: it was timed on a book held in memory and written customer after customer, and no check counted what it read. For anything that reads the deck table: (1) build the test book the way uploads lay it out (day after day, with real churn) and the real table's other indexes; (2) count the pages it touches in the transaction (`pg_stat_get_xact_blocks_fetched` for the table and its indexes: a number the cache does not change) and assert a bound, on the whole book and on half of it; (3) never say "N seconds" in a header or a Part without saying what it was measured on; (4) give the work a clock and a place to stop, write each finished unit down, and cut the script off by the database's own limit well inside what the editor waits for; (5) say what the live number is not. **(6) The same lesson a second time, the same day (handbook Part 108):** the day-by-day form was checked in pages, but on a book whose days lay together, and cost 3 seconds a day live because a day's rows are NOT together on the live table. Build the timing book in the WORST layout the table can have (every row in a random place: `create table ... as select * from ... order by md5(id::text)`), count the pages PER UNIT OF WORK (a loan number, not a day), on the whole book and on half of it, and look at the PLAN once (`explain (analyze, buffers)`): the first draft of the next form read 54 pages a loan number and every answer was right.
- **When the owner asks whether an update "fills itself", find every copy of the value first** (handbook Part 102). The registry was right and the report was not: a request keeps its own copy of the account from the moment it was asked, the report printed that copy before the registry's, a junk copy hid the real number for ever, and beyond 400 staff the registry was not read at all. Ask where else the figure is stored, which copy wins, and what happens at a size nobody tested, before answering yes.
- **A "how comes" is answered by replaying the owner's own sample through the real code, at every stage of the day, before a Part is cited** (handbook Part 136). On 23 Nov the owner asked why a cleared customer who was not on the day's initial file was on staff's lists; the answer named the all-time ghost, called it the owner's own order and offered three ways to drop it. The owner had to ask twice more. The replay (the sample's pasted rows and five neighbours, through `expdfMine`, the report, the card and the tab, before the day's files, with the morning file only, with both) took an hour and found two more faults, one of them on the phone every morning: the list was last week's file until the day's later file arrived. A Part says what a rule was meant to do; only running the sample says what the screens do. And when a rule of the owner's seems to conflict with a later one ("all time" against "the lists per day must come from initial files"), read the earlier one again in the light of the later: it was the reading that was wrong, not the owner.
- **PostgreSQL's `least()` and `greatest()` skip an empty value.** `coalesce(least(x, cap), dflt)` answers the cap when x is NULL, not the default: the first form of `call_min_secs()` read a book with no setting as two thousand million seconds (handbook Part 137). Write `least(coalesce(x, dflt), cap)`, and a check of a setting's reading always includes "no row at all", empty, and words. **And a function's cost is measured on a connection that has called it before:** the first call of a new session reads the catalogue to plan the statements inside it (about a hundred pages, once for each connection), which looked like "118 pages more" for a change that costs one. Print the first call beside the warmed one.
- **A function replaced in place says in its answer which rule it counted by** (handbook Part 137). The house rule is a new name for a corrected function; where a new name would bring back a heavy read until the file is run, the same name may be kept IF the answer carries a mark the app requires (the board's `"min"`, `'MIN:5'` on the report's number rows) and the app shows NOTHING for an answer without it. Never the old figure, never a nought.
- **A link by name needs a list of the names that do not link** (handbook Part 109). The request tab joins a call agent to the registry by an exact name; two of five were the same people under a longer name and three were not there at all, and nothing said so until the finance manager's column had blanks. Where a screen joins two lists by a spelling, ship the read-only list of the spellings that fail beside it (RUN-ME-073), and read the join in the code before answering that something "fills itself".

- **A block the owner is asked to paste stays small, flush-left, and ends with an END line** (handbook Part 110, addendum 2). On 17 Nov a 23,615-character script reached the SQL editor cut at exactly 20,000 characters, in the middle of a line, with a long run of blanks in front of that line that the text does not hold: nothing ran (the editor parses the whole text before it runs any of it) but the owner lost a round and was left to guess. What cuts a paste is not known, and scripts of 30,000 characters had run before. So: every block the owner pastes is under 10,000 characters (one block for each person, or for each step, when a script is longer: a script for one person is a few thousand characters, not the 23,000 of the general engine), every line starts at the left edge, and the last line is a comment that says END OF <name>, which the owner checks is in the editor; a script with several statements is made so that a cut at any statement boundary leaves nothing half done (the kept test cuts it at every line and at every 61st character, 215 and 118 times, and fails on a partial change). **When an error shows a LINE and a text that stops mid-word, count the characters up to it before blaming the script**: 19,967 + 33 was exactly 20,000, and that is how this was found. A fix that is only "say it again" is not a fix: the next paste can be cut the same way.

- **"The day's file" is the date, the date's own weekday and the newest upload of each team: copy all three** (handbook Part 139). Writing the stamp of `db/RUN-ME-089` I copied RUN-ME-058's "which upload wins" and left out its `weekday = p.weekday`: a file of that date filed under another weekday would have been read as the day's. It was found only because a pin of mine named a file that does not exist and I opened the real one to fix the pin. Before SQL that reads a day's deck: read `latestSnapshot`'s callers in `api/_lib/expdf.js` and RUN-ME-058 whole, put a row "of that date under another weekday" in the fixture, and pin the sentence against 058's. **And a break nothing can observe is said, not counted:** taking the "nobody was spoken to" test out of the deck read changed nothing the check could see in any plan it could force (PostgreSQL stops a join whose other side is empty); the check says why it is not in its list, and the guarantee stays in the file.
- **Before a stage is called brief, look at when the data arrives; and a cure is not finished until the screen says what it cannot show** (handbook Parts 136 and 140). Part 136 built the day's list off its own two files, called the hours with the morning file alone "the morning", and left "no note that the later file is not in yet" under Not done as something to offer. The owner's own table of upload times (`db/RUN-ME-087`) showed those hours are 05:00 to 20:00: the whole working day read *recovered 0* on every handset with no words. Ask WHEN each file comes in before deciding how long a state lasts (the answer is in the book: `min(created_at)` per day and type), and when a figure cannot be known yet, the screen says so in the same change: a dash where there is no room for words, a line where there is (CLAUDE.md section 2).
- **Tested SQL goes to the owner before the handbook is written, and a tested change is pushed before it is polished** (handbook Part 138). On 23 Nov the seven blocks of the 2025 notices were built and run at 17:46 EAT; the chat went on to write the Part; the session stopped at about 17:50 (its worker was restarted) and came back at 03:22 on 24 Nov. The owner waited nine and a half hours for SQL that was ready, and the change sat uncommitted on the chat's machine. The order of work is: test; paste (the order of 23 Nov, section 2); commit and push to the chat's branch; then the Part and this file; then the pull request.
- **Data the owner pastes proves itself, and the chat proves its own copy** (handbook Part 138). A block of rows carries the md5 of its own lines and writes nothing when it differs (the builder writes the fingerprint; the block's own SQL computes it again in the database, blanks at the line ends, Windows line ends and empty lines aside); the script that uses the rows is built for their number and writes nothing on any other. What the chat typed into its answer is not trusted either: its messages are in the session's record on its own machine (`~/.claude/projects/<project folder>/<session id>.jsonl`, written as each message ends), so the fenced blocks of the message are taken out of it and compared byte for byte with the files before the owner is told to run them. Sixty thousand characters copied by a chat are sixty thousand chances of one wrong digit, and a wrong digit in a loan number is another customer.
- **A slide for the TV is looked at as a picture before it is called done, and its layout is measured by the TEXT** (handbook Part 141). The wall's figure columns do not wrap and share the width equally, so one header too long, or one column too many, runs into its neighbour. The first cut of the legal calls slide had thirteen columns and passed a check that asked `scrollWidth`: a right-aligned cell spills to the LEFT, which `scrollWidth` does not see. Take the picture (`SHOTS=<dir>` on the kept check), read it, and measure each cell's text against the cell's own box and against its neighbour's text, at more than one width. And when a renderer is given a new kind of cell, see it drawn: the wall had never applied a column's own mark, so the red asked for on another slide weeks earlier had never been on the TV.
- **A limit that counts concurrent work takes its place where it checks, not after the work begins** (handbook Part 155). The first in-flight counter of Claudian-PMO read `inflight` before the work and added to it after: nine questions at once all passed. And **a deliberate break that breaks nothing is the same mistake in a test**: the first break of it moved the increment but left it before the delay, so the test stayed green and was reported "not caught" only because the report was read; the break was then run by hand and corrected. When a break is "not caught", run it by hand before strengthening the test: either the test is weak or the break is not a break, and the two are different repairs.
- **A request nobody has seen the provider accept is said so.** Claudian-PMO's request to Claude Haiku 5.5 (thinking off, effort low, one cached block) is built from the documented shape and a fake client has read it; the real provider has not, because a chat holds no key. The first real answer is the test, and a request the provider calls malformed (400) is written into the chat log's note with its own words so that the owner can read what to put right. Never write "works" for a call no provider has answered.
- **`npm i <anything>` removes what was installed with `--no-save`.** `playwright-core` (the browser checks, `tools/browser-checks/` and `tools/language-audit/crawl*.mjs`) is never in `package.json`: installing `acorn` for the language audit pruned it and every browser check failed with "Cannot find module" until `npm i --no-save playwright-core` put it back. After any `npm i` of something else, run that line again before a browser check.

- **A list is looked at at a laptop's width, not only at a desk's, and its cells are looked at word by word** (handbook Part 158). The Hope Ai list has thirteen columns, the last of them a long question; every check passed at 1700 pixels, and the picture at 1366 showed "PORTA L", "MANAG ER" and "TOKE NS": the core's `S.fit` lets a cell break anywhere, and a table then squeezes every short column in proportion to the one long column. Take the picture at 1366 and 1700 (`SHOTS=<dir>`), and measure the text's own boxes (a Range over the TEXT node: a chip's padding box is another box with another top and is not a second line). `S.fit = 'words'` breaks at spaces only.
- **A control made of pointer events opens on the pointer's release, and a click is only the keyboard's and the screen reader's way in** (handbook Part 156). The first form of the Claudian bubble opened on `click`; in the real-browser phone check a touch tap produced a pointer press and release and no click, so the bubble could be dragged and never opened, and no unit test could have seen it. The release is the tap, the click that may follow it is ignored for a moment, and a click with no release before it (Enter, Space, a screen reader) still does the work. **A browser check that waits for a page property waits for a property the page really sets** (`S.entered`, not the `S.user` the call page never sets: the first form read "nothing asked yet" after the eight-second timer had fired), and a fixture helper is checked by reading what it did (a helper that "set a row" for a row the fake book did not hold switched nothing on).
- **When the owner says what a thing knows, read what it is actually given, and read the older words against the code before adding new ones** (handbook Part 159). "I believe Claudian-PMO has knowledge from this chat and db": it had eight glossary lines and no card. Writing the cards, the readers checked every claim against the code and found that SEVEN statements stage 1 had written from memory were wrong (`Exp` is Expired, a portfolio call has no length, the tick ignores "Ana namba nyingine", Exp.Def is on every phone, role and ZONE, the bonus plan is a proposal, 26 is the admin's). **Whatever text a model is told is a promise: read it against the code each time it is touched, and put the number the system fixes under a test that reads the code.**
- **A picker is judged on questions its builder did not write, and a word that is ordinary in the two languages is not a topic.** The first picker counted keys: *leo* (today) brought two cards for a football question, *saturday* for a wedding speech, *habari* matched *hatari* by a slip. The weights (a key only one card has counts most; ordinary words under half), the minimum score and the slip rule came from reading what 160 questions written by strangers brought back, not from a test of mine. The one number to watch is how many questions get a card at all; the Hope Ai tab now shows which cards each real answer used.
- **A key is a trigger: judge a list of keys by what it does to questions it was not drawn from, and move a key out one at a time** (handbook Part 159, addendum 1). A key that only one card has brings that card by itself, so a function word ("nini", "what") or a short stem inside an ordinary word (*omba* in *naomba*, *chap* in *chapati*) fills the model's three lines with a card nobody needed. Let agents PROPOSE, then filter by questions that are not about the card, count other AREAS and not other cards (sharing inside an area is what the document-frequency weight is for), and measure each drop on its own: the agents' 125 drops together lost recall that three of them did not. Say what was used as a guard (a measure that decided which key to refuse is no longer a measure), and keep the questions in the repository so that the next key edit meets the same floors.
- **A delegated check is collected by what it found, never by its name.** The collector wrote `area-<name>.json` from every finished task, so a run that died on the account's usage limit (no verdicts) overwrote a verified one; it now keeps a result with verdicts over one without. And a foreground `sleep` is refused by the harness: wait for a result with a background `until` loop on the file.
- **A scope is tested through the whole path, and a break nothing notices is a missing test** (handbook Part 160). The phone leader's live scope (`scopedUser` + `scopeFor`, the teams their access code holds NOW) was covered by a unit test of the reader, and the break that dropped the phone's own record on the way in (`cu` in `callSubject`) passed every test, because the reader quietly fell back to the handset's own teams. A scope has two ends (where it is decided, where it is used): test with the two apart (the code's teams differ from the handset's), through the real handler.
- **A rule for part of a list is tested with a list that has both parts in it** (handbook Part 161). The bell's check is asked only for the promises due TODAY; my first test of "an overdue promise is not repeated" had nothing due today beside it, so the check never ran and the break "repeat the overdue ones too" passed. And a rule with a time in it is tested at the boundaries AND with the neighbour's time (two promises logged at different moments: the page is told the earliest next mark, not the latest): the first test could not tell the two apart because every mark of the clock grid coincided.
- **A check that seeds fixed times of day gives the server a clock too** (handbook Part 162). The browser check for the clear seeded chats at 05:50, 07:01 and so on of today and let the server read the real time: before 07:01 UTC the server (rightly) refused the clear's line as a time in the future, and the check passed or failed by the hour it was run. A time-dependent rule is tested with the clock the test controls (`NOWMS` in the browser check, `nowMs` in the unit tests), and a break that moves nothing the test can see (the first "deletes past the line" break: the late chat sat on a day the clear never reaches, so the days kept it, not the line) is a test that puts the evidence where only the guarded line can save it.
- **A list of keywords is measured on questions its writer did not write, in two halves, and then on a fresh batch** (handbook Part 163). The ready answers' keys were written by me, found 100% of my own questions and were far worse on strangers' (60% right on the first draft). Have other writers produce the questions (direct, near-miss, uncovered, off-topic), tune on one half and keep the other aside, and if the aside half is looked at, say its number is generous and get a fresh batch. A gap between the halves is a tuning that learned the questions. And **put the lint in before the keys grow**: a key and its plural, or a stem and its longer form, count one word twice, and a stem is a substring (`reach` in `reached`, `zima` in `nzima`).

- **A write that one person can send twice is made so that the second copy cannot exist, and the button is only the first wall** (handbook Part 164). A deduction case had a random id for every press, so sixteen presses were sixteen cases, and the reporters are paid per case. The cure is the one the phones' call sync already uses: the ids are made from what was submitted and the table's primary key refuses the second copy (`ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`), so nothing is read first and two server instances cannot race; the button that switches itself off and says it is saving is for the person's sake. **A test of that race gives ONE fake database many requests in flight: two fake databases are two books.** **A closed drawer is hidden, not removed: a browser check waits for its display, not for its content to leave the page.** **And a deletion script's fixture has a row for every column the rule compares (one that differs ONLY in that column), or a break of that column passes unseen: three of 39 did.**
- **A path that costs nothing is still a path that writes, and a table somebody reads in full needs a ceiling at every door** (handbook Part 163, addendum 1). The helper's free answers (a ready answer, a greeting, a tap) skipped the pace and every limit because they cost no model, yet each wrote a log row: one script could fill the table the owner reads. The pace is asked before the first write, whichever door the question used, and the free path has a daily ceiling counted in memory.
- **A mask is tested with the characters a person can type, and what a regular expression runs over is cut first.** `\d` does not see full-width or Arabic-Indic digits and a zero-width space splits a number; a megabyte question held the whole process and past 6 MB threw: normalise (NFKC, invisible characters out), cut to a few thousand characters, then mask.
- **A touch is followed by a mouse click aimed at whatever is under the finger THEN, and a module that is hidden at sign-out has not forgotten anyone.** A control that moves itself on a tap swallows the tail for a moment; a handset shared by two officers needs a `forget()`, a session counter so that an answer still on its way is ignored, and a browser check that signs out and looks.
- **A break runner is part of the code it breaks.** The first runner of the Claudian fixes pointed at a scratch worktree that was later removed, two of its 21 breaks named a function that had been refactored ("could not build" is a result, not a pass), and it left its `.good` copies in the repository: compare and remove them, and re-read the runner when the code under it changes. **It runs on a COPY of the repository, never on the real files** (on 25 Nov the owner interrupted the script twice while a break was applied and `present.js` was left with a changed line each time: found by checking that every break's original text was back; a copy cannot be left broken). **And its `grep` of the test output is `grep -a`:** the output of a failing test can carry bytes that make a plain `grep` print "binary file matches", which a runner reads as "no failure" and reports a catch as a miss (found on 25 Nov by running one break by hand, handbook Part 164, addendum 4).
- **Adding a column to a wall table is a layout change for every column, measured at the laptop's width.** A fixed table (`table-layout:fixed`) shares its width equally and a figure never wraps: two more columns on the PMO Collection slide pushed the week's shillings past their cell at 1366 px and below, and no unit test could see it; the real-browser check that measures each cell's content against its cell, at the five widths, did (handbook Part 164, addendum 4). Give the slide its own widths (`tcls`) and re-measure.
- **A figure that only some people may see never goes inside an answer that is cached for everyone with the same teams** (handbook Part 164, addendum 6). `cachedAnswer` keys an answer by the viewer's teams and the day (`scopeKey`), NOT by the tabs they hold, so a deduction count put into the dashboard's answer would have been served to a person who does not hold the PMO Deduction tab. Give such a figure a read of its own behind the one door's gate (`FN_TAB`), let the page ask for it only when `allowed('tab')`, and have a test read the cached answers' whole bodies to prove they never touch it.
- **A scan that proves a function does NOT do something reads the function's whole body, and its break is a real call placed where the work is** (addendum 6). The first scan read `dashboardFull`, a thin wrapper (its body is `dashboardFullCompute_`, a thousand lines further on), and a break that added the call to the compute function survived. Name the function that really computes, assert the length of what was read, and run the break in that function.
- **A rank has one author, and a test fails on a second** (handbook Part 164, addendum 7). The HOPE Calls slide's order was made by the call board's sort and only READ by the page, which is why "sort it by talk time, not calls" was one comparator on the server and one mark on the page, and not two orders to keep alike. When a list's order is a rule the owner can change, find who makes it, change it there, and pin the page against sorting it again (the slide's code, comments stripped, has no `.sort(`). **And an old-code yardstick of a refactor is kept word for word when the rule it vouches for changes on purpose:** put the new rule on top of the old list (a stable re-sort), say so in its header, and count the books where the two orders differ, so that the new key is really compared.
- **A ratio is printed with its own divisor, one period to a tile, and a test holds each figure to the dashboard's own** (handbook Part 164, addendum 8). The DAY slide's Recovered tile carried the week's recovery, "118% of uncollected" (the week's) and "TZS … today", and stood beside *Uncollected today*: the owner read the week's percentage against today's uncollected and said the pair "doesn't make sense". A figure that is a ratio says which period it is of and prints the divisor under it; the week's figure and the day's go on tiles of their own; and the facts each tile stands on are pinned on the server (the Recovered card IS the Weekly recovery trend's tile for today; the strip's Recovery % IS the total's), so that neither can drift while the other is read.

## 8. Waiting on someone

The queries (counts and messages only, no statement text, so no customer data; they read Supabase's log store, so they cost the database nothing): `select parsed.error_severity as severity, parsed.sql_state_code as code, parsed.user_name as db_user, regexp_replace(substr(event_message, 1, 160), r'[0-9]+', 'N') as message, count(*) as n from postgres_logs cross join unnest(metadata) as metadata cross join unnest(metadata.parsed) as parsed where regexp_contains(parsed.error_severity, 'ERROR|FATAL|PANIC') group by severity, code, db_user, message order by n desc limit 40` and `select request.method as method, request.path as path, response.status_code as status, count(*) as n from edge_logs cross join unnest(metadata) as metadata cross join unnest(metadata.request) as request cross join unnest(metadata.response) as response where response.status_code >= 400 group by method, path, status order by n desc limit 40` (not run against Supabase from here; Supabase changed the syntax of its API in September but says the dashboard's Logs Explorer is not affected, GitHub discussion 48235 in the supabase organisation). **Read in the code and judged unlikely, not measured:** the phone's sync (`sync()` in `call-core.js`) cleans every field (numbers through `num`, an outcome whitelist, a hashed id) and writes with `upsert ... onConflict: 'id', ignoreDuplicates`, so it makes no duplicate-key error by design. **Still open, to be read against the message when it is known:** a statement timeout (57014) on a heavy portal read, a unique violation from code that relies on catching it (`imprest.js`, `dup`), invalid input syntax from a junk parameter (22P02), a lock or deadlock from an upload. **Not done, said plainly:** no log was seen; nothing was changed; the memo times were not touched; and nothing will be changed that adds a read to `/api/upload` or `/api/call`.
